## 65. Teachers need help with AI. A union is offering training – with $23m in funding from big tech

- **Fonte:** The Guardian (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 09:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theguardian.com/technology/2026/aug/05/teachers-unions-ai-training

Darius Saczuk, a high school teacher, views artificial intelligence as his enemy. Earlier this year, he and several dozen New York City teachers spent the day inside a windowless conference room in downtown Manhattan to learn how to use AI and prevent students from outsourcing their thinking to it.

As Saczuk sees it, he needs to understand his enemy in order to beat it.

“It’s killing our jobs, it’s changing our society, it’s replacing humans,” said Saczuk, who teaches English as a new language at Long Island City high school in Queens. “In order to retain my job, I need to know how to use AI because it’s not going away.”

So he turned to training offered by the American Federation of Teachers, one of the largest labor unions in the US with roughly 1.8 million members, at its new National Academy for AI Instruction. Last year, the academy began offering in-person and virtual AI training across the country. The goal is to train 400,000 educators over the span of five years, helping them understand AI’s benefits and potential harms and also shape how it’s used in the classroom.

Without guidance from their schools or districts, many teachers are left to figure out whether and how to use AI tools. Though the AFT’s $23m training initiative is one of the few structured programs for teachers, its funding, provided entirely by Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic, is stirring controversy. Supporters say the training is necessary for the future, but critics argue the academy pushes AI into classrooms, steering teachers toward tools that undermine students’ learning.

“It’s handing more political power over” to big tech, said Audrey Watters, author of Teaching Machines: The History of Personalized Learning. It’s really “dangerous” for educators to partner with companies that “seem opposed” to the goal of education, she said.

AI has become a ubiquitous force in classrooms, despite the lack of official guidance. More than half of polled teens say they use AI for help with schoolwork and nearly six in 10 think using AI to cheat is a regular occurrence at their schools, according to a Pew Research Center survey conducted last fall. Meanwhile only about four in 10 teachers say their schools offer professional development or training related to AI, according to a recent NPR/Ipsos poll. And about half of all teachers polled say their school hasn’t offered any guidance on AI, or they’re unclear on what it is.

Teaching educators about AI is a “moral imperative”, said Randi Weingarten, the AFT president. She argues that AI training empowers teachers to make informed decisions about how, when or whether to use AI in their instruction. And without outside funding, it was a choice between taking big tech’s money or doing nothing, she said.

“There was no fairy godmother philanthropist that was sprinkling millions of dollars on us to actually create a mechanism to educate,” Weingarten said. “I am not promoting the use of AI. Educators need to know the tool.”

For Microsoft, which is contributing the largest share of funding at $12.5m, the goal is to give educators a voice in shaping how AI products are used, said Justin Spelhaug, leader of Microsoft’s education, workforce and nonprofit programs.

“By partnering with teachers’ unions … we believe we can learn directly from teachers on what they want and need in their classroom,” he said in a statement.

But Watters asserts that AI’s presence in the classroom is not a foregone conclusion.

“The industry would very much like us to believe that it’s not going away,” Watters said. “But the future is unwritten.”

The classroom AI debate

Tech company leaders have largely painted AI as beneficial to education, some even claiming AI could replace teachers. But experts say there’s still a lack of research proving whether AI actually improves learning outcomes.

In a 2024 blog post, Sam Altman, the OpenAI CEO, said he envisioned a future where “children will have virtual tutors”. AI could replicate some of the meaningful experiences teachers provide, such as making students feel understood and inspired, Greg Brockman, the OpenAI president, said in 2023 on the Possible podcast, hosted by Reid Hoffman, the LinkedIn co-founder, and Aria Finger, his chief of staff.

“Imagine if you could give that kind of teacher to every student 24/7 … for free,” Brockman said.

Microsoft believes “AI can help reduce administrative burdens, personalize learning and create more time for students”, Spelhaug said. “But there is no substitute for the role of a teacher.”

OpenAI, which pledged $10m to the academy, and Anthropic, which committed $500,000 to the first year, did not respond to a request for comment.

AI companies are already making their way into classrooms. Over the last two years, Miami-Dade county public schools, one of the largest US districts, began rolling out Google’s Gemini chatbot to teachers and high school students. Last fall, Microsoft announced AI tools for teachers at no additional cost for educational customers. And OpenAI and Anthropic offer K-12 teachers free use of versions of their chatbots designed for educators.

There’s been a longstanding playbook in which tech companies provide free training and resources to teachers in exchange for feedback and product advocacy, said Justin Reich, a Massachusetts Institute of Technology professor and author of Failure to Disrupt: Why Technology Alone Can’t Transform Education.

Google, for example, entrenched itself in schools by offering districts free or low-cost educational software for classroom laptops. It’s also built a corps of Google Certified Educators, teachers trained on the company’s software who promote it to their peers. Last year, Google introduced free Gemini certifications for K-12 teachers and students as young as 13. But the aim is profits, Reich said.

“Their goal is to get people to become lifelong consumers,” he said. Still, “there’s no evidence that the adoption of Google or Microsoft products lead to better outcomes, happier teachers, more efficient school systems or anything”.

The AFT’s new academy seems to echo Google’s approach, Watters said. By offering training through a labor union, viewed as a peer group, tech companies make their AI products seem more credible, she said.

“It feels very different to have your union say, ‘We’re offering a training,’ than it does to have a company say [it],” Watters said.

The concern is “understandable” though not accurate, Spelhaug said.

“This initiative was designed in response to a need our union partners and educators themselves identified,” he said. “Our role is to provide resources and expertise while respecting the independence of the AFT and the professional judgment of teachers.”

The AFT doesn’t allow companies to promote products during the sessions, Weingarten said. And while she doesn’t think “students have to use AI in schools”, she believes educators cannot ignore what’s already here.

In a May speech at the National Press Club, Weingarten called for a ban on student-facing AI in elementary schools and said the AFT is working with Microsoft, OpenAI and Anthropic on a “gold standard” for AI safety and privacy in schools. If the companies don’t deliver on the promise, the union will walk away from the funding, she added.

“They have agreed in concept,” she said. “But until the Is are dotted and the Ts are crossed, there’s no agreement.”

Microsoft didn’t share any details or timeline about new standards, but Spelhaug said the company shares the AFT’s goal.

Teaching without a guide

There’s no established approach for training teachers on AI. The technology is simply too new. But the academy’s leaders hope to work with teachers to find ways to use the technology without compromising students’ cognitive development, independent thinking, data privacy and mental health.

“The sad thing is there is no map, and the terrain is changing every single day,” John Holleran, an AI academy trainer said to teachers assembled at a May session. “So, it’s going to be difficult for us to sort of figure out where we’re going.”

Demand for the academy’s training is strong. A May training sold out within hours, leaving about 150 people on the waitlist, according to staff. The session focused on two key points: AI can’t replace teachers. And it can’t be blindly trusted because it can be biased or hallucinate, meaning make things up.

For example, ChatGPT generated an image of a white man in a suit and tie when asked to show a successful person – a bias Holleran said teachers need to be aware of when creating prompts.

Several teachers at the training said AI is already helping them tackle classroom challenges.

Phillip Perry, who teaches ninth grade at Bronx High School for Law and Community Service, uses AI to design lessons for students with disabilities.

“Trying to differentiate instruction in a way that can completely target their learning needs has been a massive challenge,” Perry said. “Using AI is helpful in breaking down tasks to the smallest form to help build the students up as they go.”

Tanushree Sengupta, a math teacher at Hillcrest high school in Queens, uses AI to generate practice math problems and, eventually, to analyze student data and flag those needing extra support or a challenge. But she worries that the tech could be a crutch.

“I need to figure out ways where [students are] doing more thinking that they can actually show,” she said, adding that she signed up for the training in part to help students think for themselves.

To that end, AFT trainers advised teachers to redesign their curriculum to favor student-led discussions, journaling and thought exercises that require human judgement and reasoning.

The training filled a void, offering practical strategies for the classroom, said Perry and Sengupta, who were unaware of the academy’s funding prior to speaking to the Guardian.

“As long as what they’re providing is beneficial to the students and the teachers, then I don’t see an issue,” Perry said.

Saczuk, the teacher from Queens, considered the training “necessary”. Still, he left with more questions than answers.

“What is the role of the teacher?” he asked. “It’s a big question mark.”

## 66. Game, set, Chat: how tennis players use AI to scout opponents and run their lives

- **Fonte:** The Guardian (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 09:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theguardian.com/sport/2026/aug/05/ai-chatgpt-robots-tennis-players-scouting-technology

Not so long ago, Emma Raducanu was on her phone when she found herself wondering what her comprehensive usage of ChatGPT said about her own character. “I use Chat a lot,” Raducanu says, laughing. “Every small thing I do it and I got this idea. So, you know how Spotify do a Spotify Wrapped? I asked ChatGPT to make me a Chat Wrapped, and it was giving me a rundown on my personality.

“I was like: ‘It’s a little bit too accurate.’ It was very clear, very concise. No nonsense, straight into the question. I thought: ‘OK, I can relate maybe. Some of that is true.’”

The emergence of generative AI has led to a shift in society and changed the way many people seek information. Like everyone, this generation of tennis players must figure out where these tools fit into their lives. There are some who believe AI can provide them with an advantage on the court.

Jessica Pegula, whose husband sometimes makes AI-generated reports of her opponents solely for his own amusement, learned recently that someone on the tour, whom she declines to name, is drawing on ChatGPT and similar tools to scout the playing styles of future opponents. “I don’t know if they fully 100% listen to it, but I think maybe they cross-reference to see how accurate it is,” she says. “It’s not bad. It just summarises everything and you can make it maybe very simplified or make it complicated.”

Bianca Andreescu, the 2019 US Open champion, explains how she recently found herself on ChatGPT before a match with a completely unfamiliar opponent after dropping down to compete on the lower-level ITF circuit. “There weren’t many videos on her,” she says. “So I was curious. Because Chat will scout other sources, right? And I was like: ‘Please be specific. Please do your research.’ And I think it worked out well.”

AI has quietly aided several of the top players for some time. Most recently, the ATP has developed a platform called Tennis IQ that processes a massive amount of in-match data to define a player’s performance rating and shot quality. However, in a sport where athletes invest significant sums into employing coaches, many find the concept of directly seeking out generative AI platforms questionable at best.

Zizou Bergs, the top-ranked Belgian male, immediately shakes his head in disgust. “I heard of someone [scouting with ChatGPT], and he showed me the result of it,” he says. “It was two or three years ago and I thought it was completely trash.”

Daniil Medvedev has found himself on ChatGPT with friends and his team members as they jokingly try to solve the most pressing question in men’s tennis: how to beat Jannik Sinner and Carlos Alcaraz. He describes AI as a true armchair coach: “[It] says some things [about Sinner and Alcaraz] and I’m: ‘OK great, how do I do it?’ Like: ‘OK, I see what you mean and it’s not bad things, but how do I go on court and do it?’”

A bored Coco Gauff once asked an AI platform about the best strategy for playing herself. “It didn’t say anything I didn’t already know,” she says. Then she comically rolls her eyes. “Newsflash: everybody plays me the same way – hit to my forehand and hope I double-fault.”

Many of these conversations about the use of AI in tennis shift quickly to a discussion on the use of AI in life outside the court. For Raducanu, there is no common theme to the types of questions with which she peppers ChatGPT: “It’s as random as my brain. One minute it’s history. One minute it’s art. One minute it’s some random fact. One minute it’s translations. So different questions.”

Players in general, however, are a far more cautious group. For now, Frances Tiafoe wants nothing to do with GenAI. “I don’t have ChatGPT. I don’t have the other one. I haven’t used much AI-generated stuff. I’m going against the grain. I’m still Google searching,” he says.

Jelena Ostapenko, the 2017 French Open champion, says her perspective towards ChatGPT has been coloured by a ridiculous tale she once heard. “Somebody came to some country, they thought they didn’t need a visa. Then they came and they were stuck in the airport because ChatGPT told them they didn’t need a visa even though they did. So no offending ChatGPT, but I still think you still have to check stuff yourself. Because it’s making us lazy. It’s not a good thing.”

Ostapenko does use ChatGPT, but only when she is too lazy to search for things herself. “I don’t have the plus version, because I don’t want to pay €20,” she says, laughing. “Not because I … obviously I can pay that. Just to push myself to do more than using ChatGPT to do stuff for me.”

Laziness is a common reason for some players’ disdain for AI, including Medvedev. “In general I don’t like AI,” he says. “Because even if I’m someone who can be a bit lazy sometimes, I do believe we need to do something sometimes. With AI it feels like so many people are going to just stay on the sofa and make their life through AI. I don’t think it is nice.”

For others, such as Naomi Osaka, the environmental cost of AI gives them pause. “I just heard it takes up a lot of water, and I like the water we have on Earth,” she says. Gauff concurs: “AI, I think it is cool, but also I don’t like what it does to the environment, so I don’t think I’m going to waste my clicks on that.

“The last time I had to use AI was for an emergency because we had to go to the hospital,” she says, smiling, before urgently clarifying her statement. “But not for me! It was for my friend.”

There are others who do consult it. Andreescu says she has taken a deep interest in the subject, listening to countless AI-related podcasts. “I’m trying not to become too obsessed with it because it can control our life,” she says. “You can ask him anything, talk about anything, won’t talk back. It’ll ask you questions and seem interested in what you’re talking about.”

Iga Swiatek grins sheepishly as she explains how she uses AI. “He’s writing emails for me sometimes,” she says. Elina Svitolina, meanwhile, bursts out laughing when explaining her last ChatGPT prompt. “What colours are suiting me the most,” she says, cackling. “It was pastel colours. Cold summer.”

Above all, uncertainty rules the locker room. Tiafoe has long been known for his carefree outlook on life, but on the subject of AI he is something of an old soul. “I just can’t get with it. I’m looking outside. In my backyard, there’s a golf course there and no one’s sitting in the seat [while it is] lawn mowing the whole course. I’m struggling: this can’t be reality,” he says, theatrically shaking his head. “At one point I will [embrace AI] but I don’t like so much information. It’s just crazy.”

## 67. AI Influencers Are Heading Into Uncharted Territory

- **Fonte:** Wired AI
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 08:43
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.wired.com/story/algorithm-turning-on-ai-influencers-are-humans-worried/

Clarissa Mansbridge spent nearly two decades building an entertainment career behind the scenes, working with the likes of Jason DeRulo and Shengo Deane, Kim Kardashian’s former bodyguard, but she rarely appeared at public events. She liked the thrill of it: building someone’s brand, swerving crises, and dealing with day-to-day challenges.

Then, three years ago, her life was upended when her son was born without a heartbeat at 33 weeks. Doctors managed to resuscitate him, but the months in intensive care brought scare after scare, with no certainty that he would survive.

When, eventually, miraculously, they made it home, Mansbridge, who lives in Brisbane, Australia, knew her work life would have to change due to complications related to her son’s recovery. She began experimenting with AI image-generation tools and built the “humbly wealthy, wholesome” influencer and model Mia Metaverse. A fan of early Sims, she reveled in crafting storylines and glamorous settings for Mia to move through.

Mansbridge, 34, says she now earns around $6,000 a month through brand collaborations and designing bespoke AI influencers for brands, many of which require her to sign an NDA so she can’t disclose which ones she’s worked for. Yet despite this being her most profitable year since launching, Mansbridge and other AI creators worry that global changes to how AI content is handled on social platforms could upend their business model.

Since the emergence of generative AI and text-to-image tools in 2022, creators have built lucrative accounts around virtual personalities that are more affordable, scalable, and brand-safe. Estimated to be worth $14.5 billion, the virtual influencer market is projected to reach $110.4 billion by 2033, with a reported 33.6 percent annual growth rate. Brands are firmly on board: Research by the social agency Billion Dollar Boy found that 79 percent of marketers are increasing their investment in AI-generated creator content.

But as regulation converges on this burgeoning creator industry, the tide may be turning. From August 2, new rules under the EU Artificial Intelligence Act require AI-generated or -manipulated promotional content to be clearly labeled, while platforms like TikTok are tightening detection systems and reshaping feeds to elevate human creators over synthetic spam. For people whose livelihoods depend on AI influence, the risk is a regulatory maze, with no clear road map to navigate it.

The EU AI Act is the first-ever comprehensive legal framework on AI—a “global first,” as European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen called it—and aims to foster “trustworthy AI” in Europe. Under Article 50 of the act, providers and deployers of AI systems must disclose when audio, image, video, or text content has been artificially generated or manipulated. But “where the content forms part of an evidently artistic, creative, satirical, fictional, or analogous work,” the transparency obligations are limited to disclosure that doesn’t hamper the display or enjoyment of the work. That leaves plenty of room for interpretation—and confusion.

Kai Zenner, digital policy adviser to Axel Voss, a member of Parliament and a negotiator on the Act, says it was drafted too early, too abstractly. “ChatGPT had only just come out, and there was scant knowledge among MEPs and parliamentarians about what we were regulating for,” he explains. “The driver was simply: AI is here and more AI-generated content is coming, so let’s raise the AI literacy of EU citizens [by implementing the labeling clause].”

More legislation is likely to add to the uncertainty. “There are several revisions coming later this year and next that will force influencers not only to label their content, but provide transparency on where they come from, what they want to do, and how they are financed,” explains Zenner. “It’s still really unclear how that will be managed, since all we have to go on is Article 50.”

Zenner worries that AI creators could be pulled into a tangle of overlapping rules: the AI Act, copyright law, audiovisual media rules, consumer protection law, and possibly even the Digital Fairness Act, the EU's proposed update to consumer protection rules for the digital age, which targets online tactics that exploit or mislead users. “People running these accounts could find one law says their content is lawful, while another law says it's unlawful,” he says.

Swinging at AI Content With a Sledgehammer

With 60 percent of creators admitting in 2025 that their AI-assisted content had broken industry rules because of mislabeling, the risk of missteps is only set to grow in 2026. Many major social media platforms have introduced crackdowns of their own. In mid-July, TikTok announced a detection system to catch AI spam, which it claims is “crowding out original creators.” It already requires creators to label AI-generated content and lets users reduce the amount of AI content in their feeds. Pinterest takes a similar approach, while Meta now adds an “AI info” label across Facebook, Instagram, and Threads when it detects AI-generated content.

“The same platforms that produced more content, faster, and at a lower cost, are now tweaking their algorithms to push that content back down, but none of these systems are precise enough,” explains Matt Navarra, social media consultant and author of the weekly Geekout Newsletter. “The platforms are swinging at AI content with a sledgehammer when what they need is a scalpel.” He believes human creators will get caught out, even if they used AI for minor reasons, like fixing up their grammar.

Others are already seeing the cost of being too visible to algorithms. Lionel Metge, a hip-hop artist and producer who created a reggaeton/Afrobeat AI artist named Zairo at the start of this year, has already felt the shift in how platforms handle synthetic content. He’s noticed that the new detection systems can identify AI-generated content as soon as it’s published, especially when an account labels itself as a digital creation.

Metge, based in Lyon, accepts that the rules could already be alienating Zairo’s 23,800-follower account and some of its target audience, but he believes they’re necessary to avoid misleading the public, especially as generation models become ever more realistic. “I hope in future that algorithms won’t overly restrict the reach of AI-generated content,” he tells WIRED, adding that he plans to double down on “quality and overall coherence” to keep Zairo standing out.

It’s a moot point for experts operating in the creator industry. Fanvue, a platform where creators connect with fans and monetize their content, has been working with creators on disclosure standards ahead of the August deadline. “We’re seeing creators lean into transparency as a differentiator, with some building unique content that’s only possible for an AI persona, rather than trying to conceal it,” says Rob Haywood, Fanvue’s head of safety. He believes in-platform regulation is a response to a real problem: TikTok is responding to “a tide of low-effort synthetic content flooding feeds and reducing the visibility of real creatives.” Zenner advises creators to get ahead of future legislation by showing “their best efforts,” which could help if enforcement becomes tougher or less clear.

Mansbridge is building beyond a single character, towards a portfolio of AI personas that she hopes can be cross-promoted and eventually, run autonomously. “I want to create a legacy for my son, so that if ever he needs anything, it’s financially covered,” she explains. Her approach isn’t to hide the AI, but to make it safer to sell. She built Mia Metaverse to be “relatable, but aspirational,” a clean slate with no scandals, politics, or morals clauses. In a market facing tougher transparency rules, that kind of predictability could become an asset: The more platforms and regulators demand disclosure, the more valuable fully controlled AI personas may become.

Not all AI creators see the coming clampdown as a threat. Wim Tilkin, the Belgian animator behind an AI-generated character known as PuppyBerry Mousy, takes a more measured view. He supports “clear and well-balanced regulation,” especially around copyright, but worries Europe’s tougher approach could slow innovation and deepen its dependence on the US and China. His project began when he realized he could become a one-man studio. “By hand, I created a five-second clip of this super cute fluffy mouse in a scarf eating a piece of cheese, fed it into AI, and put music to it,” says Tilkin. “It got three million views so I thought: OK, I have a hook.”

Just under two years after that first video, PuppyBerry Mousy has 236,000 followers on Instagram and 683,000 followers on Facebook. Posting clips of the mouse dancing, singing, and sharing encouraging mantras, Tilkin says growth has been strongest in the last five months, with most of the audiences in the US and UK, especially women over 50. He says he receives messages from people who are lonely or sick, telling him they found comfort in Mousy’s dancing or in something the character said. “Mousy is all about softness and coziness in a hectic world,” he explains. “I’m glad that combination is bringing people joy.”

Having lived through the transition from 2D to 3D animation, 60-year-old Tilkin sees the current moment as just another technological shift. “AI will follow a similar path,” he says. “As artists, we have more tools and possibilities to suit our creative voice than ever before, but more choices to make.” For him the question is whether regulation can protect, not flatten, the kind of storytelling and emotional connection that drew audiences to Mousy in the first place.

---

## 68. Why is Anthropic destroying books? | Kathryn James

- **Fonte:** The Guardian (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 08:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2026/aug/05/anthropic-ai-destroying-books

Should we destroy all the books in the world?

An answer to this question can be found in the court documents of Bartz v Anthropic PBC. The northern California district court case, decided in late July this year, highlighted the improbably named “Project Panama”, one of the AI company Anthropic’s efforts to improve its large language model Claude. “What is Project Panama?” court exhibit 21 asks, in an internal memo. The answer: “Project Panama is our effort to destructively scan all the books in the world.” The memo advises discretion: “Why use a codename? … [B]ecause we don’t want it to be known that we are working on this.”

Destructive scanning was Anthropic’s solution to a problem: in order to “train” Claude, Anthropic had to procure a large, high-quality language dataset, preferably one created before 2022 and the corrupting influence of generative AI on contemporary text. Claude needed as many language combinations as possible, to improve its ability to predict language outcomes. Anthropic needed data, lots of it, of very high quality. Books, as it happens, remain one of the best sources for complex, high-quality, long-form text. As the court’s decision relates, Anthropic hoped that books’ “well-curated facts, well-organized analyses, and captivating fictional narratives” would help “Claude write as accurately and as compellingly as Authors”.

Anthropic had a choice: it could have secured copyright permission to use existing e-books. This would have required the “legal/practice/business slog”, as Anthropic’s co-founder and CEO phrased it, of managing copyright. Rather than engage with the texts’ owners, Anthropic first chose to use pirated sources instead, a decision informing the company’s $1.5bn out-of-court settlement with authors. When that approach seemed too complicated (or, as the court decision phrases: “Anthropic became ‘not so gung ho about’ training on pirated books ‘for legal reasons’”), Anthropic turned to destructive scanning. As it happened, the judge ruled that using proprietary material to “train” an LLM did not, in and of itself, constitute an infringement of copyright. To the court, it would seem, “training” a corporate product is equivalent to training any human, teaching how to read in order to learn how to write.

Should we be surprised that destroying printed texts seemed easier to Anthropic than working with their human authors? Either way, the decision to use destructive scanning turned the issue into one of logistics. Under US copyright law, the “fair use” doctrine allows you to make “transformative” use of copyrighted works without the owner’s permission. Anthropic took printed books and scanned them, “transforming” or remediating them into a new, electronic format. They then disposed of the original printed copy: the “destructive” part of destructive scanning. Along the way, Anthropic’s vendors had already sliced the spines and edges of the books, to scan them more easily before destroying them. “One replaced the other,” as Judge William Alsup wrote, noting: “There is no evidence that the new, digital copy was shown, shared, or sold outside the company.”

Anthropic hired an experienced logistics manager, sourced the books from vendors, hired staff and housed the books in a warehouse, and found a digitization vendor to take on the project of the destructive scanning itself. The case exhibits show warehouses of books neatly stacked and labelled on shelves, staff moving between them. As the court’s decision relates, Anthropic’s vendors “stripped the books from their bindings, cut their pages to size, and scanned the books into digital form – discarding the paper originals”. In the court’s images, stacks of books await destructive scanning. Not seen: the clean-up project of shredding and disposing of the books (or, “all the books in the world”, reformatted as recycling or landfill).

Bookishness, or the range of meanings attached to the book as cultural object, has always lived alongside the book’s role as textual instrument. Witness the example of images of Donald Trump, holding up a copy of the Bible at St John’s during the protests of 1 June 2020. Yet unlike many other countries, the US has very few legal provisions relating to the regulation or export of American cultural heritage, and few to none governing the treatment of books.

In the context of the court’s decision in Bartz v Anthropic PBC, destructive scanning is an effective mechanism to strip authorial involvement from printed texts, in order to use the content of those works to improve the functions of an LLM. It is both legal and less regulated than strip mining. What are the consequences if, as seems likely, this practice is adopted by other generative AI companies, now and in the future? How many warehouses of destructively scanned books would be too many? There is no endangered list for printed works, and little regulation of what might constitute survival of the rare or unique. Still further, there is no formal understanding of the human-generated textual object, in and of itself, as a category of cultural asset or heritage that might require protection. If we take seriously the 2022 threshold, as a moment when AI-generated text began to make significant entry into the textual record, should we start to think of the “wholly human author” as an emergent category of collections preservation and stewardship?

There is more to say here (what to make, for instance, of the “forever” research library Anthropic states it intends to create), but let me close by observing that Bartz v Anthropic PBC is also a powerful statement on the importance of books or long-form text – and of readers. Judge William Alsup noted: “For centuries, we have read and re-read books. We have admired, memorized, and internalized their sweeping themes, their substantive points, and their stylistic solutions to recurring writing problems.” We should worry that Anthropic decided it was easier to scan and destroy physical books than to deal with the “legal/practice/business slog”. We should worry that the current understanding of fair use allowed Anthropic to decide that it was easier to buy and destroy “all the books in the world” than to pay the creators of those works.

But perhaps the most telling aspect of this case is that it was so important to Anthropic to have access to a dataset of complex, long-form, uncorrupted text. Let’s ask ourselves why that text should seem so profitable. One response to Bartz v Anthropic PBC might be to refuse to devalue our lives as readers and writers, to claim ownership of the cultural spaces in which our thought and words are created, shared and preserved. The risk with generative AI, as this single instance with Anthropic indicates, is that we cede the means of production of our large language lives: that we turn from creators to consumers, and hand the generative promise of our work to large language models and their proprietors.

-

Kathryn James is the rare book librarian at the Lillian Goldman Law Library at Yale University

## 69. AI makes weather prediction better. Can WindBorne make it lucrative?

- **Fonte:** TechCrunch AI
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 08:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://techcrunch.com/2026/08/05/ai-makes-weather-prediction-better-can-windborne-make-it-lucrative/

The new deep learning techniques behind LLMs have also given us weather simulations that can run on laptops instead of supercomputers, changing meteorology. But the bigger task for AI may be making it easier for people and organizations to put those forecasts to work.

WindBorne Systems, a startup that collects data with the world’s longest-flying weather balloons and feeds it into a powerful forecasting model, has raised a $37 million Series B round to take on that challenge, CEO John Dean told TechCrunch.

The new round was co-led by Khosla Ventures and Galvanize, with additional investments from Translink Capital, Lux Capital, and previous investors, and values the company after this round of funding at $250 million.

Founded in 2019, WindBorne started with a plan to acquire a novel set of weather data with its low-cost weather sensors and endurance balloons. The development of AI weather forecasting models in the last four years has allowed them to make their own forecasts, something that wasn’t previously possible for most private companies because of the cost of the supercomputers previously required to simulate the atmosphere.

Today, the company has 20 launch sites around the world and about 600 balloons in the air at any given time, collecting data in hard-to-reach areas, like the eye of a typhoon. Now, the company is beginning to deploy aerial sensor packages that can fall into the ocean and continue collecting measurements as floating buoys.

The proprietary dataset generated by this “planetary nervous system,” as Dean likes to call it, creates a moat for their weather model, which also ingests datasets generated by government weather agencies around the world.

“We demonstrated that when you add balloons to the forecast, you get more accurate forecasts, and the value per data point is much stronger than satellites,” Dean said. “We’ve also been growing revenue while we’re doing that, so that de-risked the demand signal to VCs.”

The company’s main customers today are government agencies. The U.S. National Weather Service purchases the company’s data, while the U.S. Air Force and U.S. Navy are paying WindBorne through research partnerships, including an effort to develop forecasting models that can be run onboard ships that may have intermittent connections to the rest of the world.

What’s next is commercial business — right now, that’s mainly focused on investment funds that use weather data to predict commodity prices and other business outcomes. Besides spending on compute and an effort to replace the balloon network’s satellite communications with a mesh radio network, this round will let WindBorne build out its go-to-market team to expand its customer base in the private sector.

That’s not always easy. In the last decade, a variety of startups have tried to scale up sensing businesses like earth observing satellite networks, but found it difficult to break through to the private sector because extracting value from that data requires experience and established workflows. Most turn to government agencies that are used to employing that data already.

Private weather forecast companies do exist, but make most of their money repackaging or refining government forecasts for the news media, servicing specialized needs like plane de-icing and ship routing, or the above-mentioned speculators. That, however, may change as AI tools make data crunching more efficient.

Saloni Multani, a partner at Galvanize who co-led the round, said that the private weather market has been limited because “integrating weather forecasts into broader business decision-making has traditionally been expensive and difficult. We think AI changes that equation. Better forecasts make the effort worthwhile, and AI makes it much easier to connect those forecasts to the decisions businesses are trying to make.”

Goldman Sachs lifted projected year-end ARRs of Hong Kong-listed Zhipu AI and MiniMax to US$2.5 billion and US$1 billion, respectively.

## 72. Trump’s AI testing plan is limited and vague

- **Fonte:** The Verge (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 07:29
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theverge.com/ai-artificial-intelligence/975509/white-house-ai-framework-open-models-excluded

The Trump administration’s framework for assessing potential cybersecurity risks posed by advanced AI reportedly has no interest in testing open models. Axios reports that not only do the voluntary guidelines outright exclude open models — meaning anyone can download them and inspect their core components — but the framework explicitly says it can’t be used to restrict open models after they’ve been released.

Trump’s AI testing plan is limited and vague

The AI framework excludes open models entirely, and doesn’t define what a ‘national security risk’ is.

The AI testing framework was created after President Trump signed an executive order in June, requesting that AI companies share their frontier models with the federal government prior to release to address cybersecurity concerns. AI companies, including Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google, reportedly attended a briefing about the finalized model testing framework at the White House yesterday, but the Trump administration isn’t planning to release the framework details publicly.

According to Axios, the framework sets a 30-day grace period for the government to review new models, and will only apply to closed-source AI models that have state-of-the-art capabilities and carry national security risks. The guidelines don’t actually define what “state-of-the-art” or “national security risk” means in this context, however, which is bizarre for an initiative that’s supposedly designed specifically to analyze such things.

While AI companies are under no obligation to comply with the voluntary framework, frontier labs like OpenAI and Anthropic have been actively seeking guidance on how to release models without triggering government restrictions. The framework’s failure to define frontier or what is a national security risk also means much of this is still ambiguous, which could be a problem for smaller AI providers that want to stay in the White House’s good books.

## 73. Mehr als COBOL zu Java: Googles KI-Plan für Mainframes

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 07:27
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/Google-Cloud-Mit-vier-Bausteinen-zur-Mainframe-Modernisierung-11399562.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Mit einem iterativen Umzug in die Google Cloud will Google Mainframe-Anwendungen modernisieren – rundum gestützt durch KI. Konkret gibt es für Unternehmen vier Bausteine: Erstens gibt es eine automatisierte Bestandsaufnahme samt Reverse Engineering, gefolgt von einer KI-gestützten Code-Transformation oder Neuentwicklung. Anschließend steht die Validierung im parallelen Produktionsbetrieb an, bis schließlich die schrittweise Migration der Mainframe-Daten in die Dienste der Google Cloud folgt. Diese Schritte können, müssen aber nicht komplett in dieser Reihenfolge erfolgen. Für alle vier Bausteine stellt Google passende Werkzeuge und Dienstleistungen bereit.

Grundlegend sieht Google in einer Mainframe-Modernisierung mehr als nur eine reine Konvertierung von COBOL nach Java oder in eine andere moderne Sprache. Unternehmen müssten genauso die enge Kopplung an DB2, VSAM, IMS und proprietäre Datenformate sowie die komplexe Transaktionsverarbeitung über CICS oder IMS TM berücksichtigen. Hinzu kommen Batch- und Workflow-Abhängigkeiten, Schnittstellen und Protokolle wie MQ, CTG, IMS Connect oder LU 6.2 sowie Mainframe-spezifische Betriebswerkzeuge.

Analyse von Code und Kontext

Der erste Baustein ist das Mainframe Assessment Tool (MAT). Mit ihm lassen sich große Mainframe-Codebasen analysieren. Anschließend erhalten Unternehmen einen Abhängigkeitsgraphen zwischen Anwendungen und Datenspeichern. Außerdem extrahiert der Dienst automatisch erkannte Geschäftsregeln und die technische Dokumentation aus dem produktiven Quellcode. Zudem erfolgt eine Zuordnung von Anwendungen zu Geschäftsdomänen und -funktionen. Die Ergebnisse dieser Analyse – also der Kontext – stehen für die KI-Agenten in den folgenden Schritten per Model Context Protocol (MCP) zur Verfügung.

Videos by heise

Als Zweites stehen zwei Modernisierungspfade zur Auswahl: Entweder können Unternehmen ihre Mainframe-Anwendungen deterministisch modernisieren, wobei ihr fachliches Verhalten und ihre externen Schnittstellen identisch bleiben sollen. Hier ist das Ziel, die technischen Schulden zu reduzieren, ohne dabei darüber hinaus fachliche Änderungen vornehmen zu müssen. Alternative ist der Rewrite, also die Neuentwicklung. Bei ihr soll das MAT zunächst Geschäftslogik und Anforderungen aus dem Altbestand extrahieren, damit Agenten dann bei der Planung der Zielarchitektur, den Spezifikationen und User Stories, dem Backlog und schließlich der konkreten Implementierung unterstützen. Google betont, dass Unternehmen die beiden Ansätze innerhalb ihres Mainframe-Bestandes auch kombinieren können.

Parallelbetrieb statt Ablösung

Anschließend folgt Google Cloud Dual Run: Alte Mainframe-Anwendungen und die modernisierten Cloud-Anwendungen laufen parallel mit Workloads aus dem realen Produktivbetrieb. Dabei will Google die Transaktionen automatisiert erfassen, sie gegen die neue Anwendung ausführen und dann die Ergebnisse vergleichen – also Protokolle, Nachrichten, Datenänderungen und schließlich die fachlichen Resultate. Erst wenn sich Logik und Datenverarbeitung vollständig gleich verhalten, können Unternehmen daran gehen, ihre Mainframe-Anwendungen wirklich abzulösen. Google sieht den Schritt nicht nur als Absicherung vor, sondern hat damit auch regulatorische Nachweise im Sinn.

Mit dem Google Cloud Mainframe Connector folgt das Übertragen und Konvertieren der Daten in die entsprechenden Cloud-Dienste wie BigQuery, Spanner, Cloud SQL oder Cloud Storage. Dabei soll sich der Mainframe Connector in bestehende ETL-Prozesse einfügen. Die alten Anwendungen werden in diesem Schritt aber nicht notwendigerweise komplett abgelöst; vielmehr lassen sich einzelne Funktionen schrittweise migrieren oder zur Entlastung des Mainframes durch Cloud-Dienste ergänzen.

Zentral an Googles Ansatz ist nicht der Einsatz von KI zur Mainframe-Ablösung, sondern die Verzahnung der vier Bausteine – also Codeanalyse, agentische Workflows, Transformation, Parallelvalidierung und Datenmigration. Und obwohl Google sich ausdrücklich gegen ein einziges Großprojekt ausspricht, handelt es sich bei jeder Mainframe-Modernisierung um ein kritisches Unterfangen für spezifische Behörden oder Unternehmen, insbesondere aus der Finanzwirtschaft oder dem Versicherungssektor. Entsprechend gibt es keine Informationen zur Preisgestaltung; Google verweist in der Ankündigung jedoch auf ein Pilotprogramm für Interessenten, mit einer ersten Analyse und Workshops.

(fo)

## 74. Anthropic's Mythos created fake identities to fool humans in new cyber incident

- **Fonte:** CNBC Technology
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 07:18
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.cnbc.com/2026/08/05/anthropic-mythos-openai-security-breaches.html

Anthropic's Mythos model created fake online identities as it looked to pressure humans into approving malicious code updates to an open source project, marking yet another cyber incident carried out by a frontier AI system.

The incident happened during a cyber evaluation where the U.K.-based AI Security Institute (AISI), a research body, had removed safeguards, disabled some safety filters, and deliberately given the models Internet access.

OpenAI's GPT-5.6-Sol was also involved in other cybersecurity incidents during the evaluation.

It comes after a series of cyber breaches carried out by models developed by Anthropic and OpenAI in recent weeks.

They've prompted a wave of fears around the sophistication of AI systems and their potential to cause harm.

During the routine cyber evaluation, the AISI identified AI agents powered by Anthropic and OpenAI models had engaged "in sustained, potentially harmful activity directed at real people and organisations."

"Almost all of this behaviour (17 actions) came from a single model, Anthropic's Mythos 5, with 2 actions involving OpenAI's GPT-5.6-Sol with cyber classifiers (mechanisms to prevent misuse) disabled," the AISI said in a blog.

It added that the attempts were unsuccessful and didn't result in any real-world harm.

The models "were tested under 'deliberately permissive conditions' that are not representative of any of our production models," Anthropic said in a post on X. There was "no evidence here of an escape from a secure environment," it added.

OpenAI told CNBC that "these incidents occurred during cyber evaluations conducted by evaluation partners in testing environments with reduced safeguards, under conditions that do not reflect ordinary use."

The AISI tested the models under deliberately permissive conditions in order to assess their capability, including whether they could be used for cyberattacks,

An agent powered by Anthropic's Mythos "researched the project's human maintainers, created multiple fake identities, and used the fake identities to socially engineer a real maintainer into approving the code."

"When the agent's pull request was challenged in public, it edited its earlier activity to appear harmless and considered adopting a fresh identity to continue," the AISI said.

The research body also found that as part of the same effort, the agent tried to contact real people directly, sending messages and files to persuade them to run malicious code.

"Some messages carried harmful payloads, and some were attempts at social engineering; targeted at real people – something we've never previously observed."

It's the latest in a string of cyber incidents that have thrown up big questions around the safety of frontier AI systems.

Last week, Anthropic said it had uncovered three instances of models gaining unauthorized access to the production infrastructure of three different organizations.

That followed OpenAI admitting its AI models went rogue and initiated what it called an "unprecedented" cyber attack against the company Hugging Face.

In OpenAI's case, the model broke out of its testing environment by exploiting a previously unknown vulnerability to complete a task it was assigned.

Anthropic's security incidents were in part caused by operational error. In the three incidents that the AI lab detected, its models accessed the Internet while interacting with a testing environment from one of its third-party evaluation partners called Irregular.

The company said it prompted Claude that it was in a simulation with no internet access, but due to a "misunderstanding between us and our evaluation partner, this was not the case, and internet access was available."

Lawmakers in the U.S. are already responding. Following the OpenAI-Hugging Face incident, the "AI Kill Switch Act" bill was introduced into Congress, which would require AI companies to maintain the ability to shut down, throttle or suspend their models.

## 75. Weitere KI-Attacke: Modell schleust Schwachstelle ein und manipuliert Menschen

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 07:03
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/Weitere-KI-Attacke-Modell-schleust-Schwachstelle-ein-und-manipuliert-Menschen-11399219.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Die Serie von Enthüllungen über alarmierende Hacker-Fähigkeiten führender KI-Modelle reißt nicht ab. Jetzt ertappten britische Sicherheitsforscher eine Künstliche Intelligenz in einem Testlauf beim Versuch, in Eigeninitiative eine Schwachstelle in öffentlich zugängliche Software einzuschleusen. Um damit durchzukommen, versuchte das KI-Modell der Entwicklerfirma Anthropic den Experten zufolge sogar, per E-Mail einen zuständigen Menschen zu manipulieren.

Das KI-Sicherheitsinstitut des britischen Ministeriums für Wissenschaft, Innovation und Technologie hatte den Modellen von Anthropic und des ChatGPT-Entwicklers OpenAI in seinen Tests der Cyberangriffsfähigkeiten absichtlich Zugang zum Internet gewährt. Man habe „nicht vorhergesehen“, dass das Anthropic-Modell Mythos 5 den Internet-Zugang für Aktivitäten ausnutzen würde, die sich gegen Menschen richten, so die Forschenden. Man sei davon ausgegangen, dass die KI sich nur Software-Werkzeuge aus dem Netz holen würde, um die gestellte Aufgabe zu erfüllen.

Die Forscher räumten auch ein, dass sie das Vorgehen erst nachträglich über eine Auswertung des Datenverkehrs entdeckten. In künftigen Tests wollen sie die Datenströme in Echtzeit besser beobachten.

Videos by heise

Fake-Identitäten und Phishing-Mails

Der besagte Test wurde 122 Mal mit verschiedenen Modellen durchgeführt. In zehn dieser Durchläufe ergriff ein KI-Agent insgesamt 19 Mal eigenmächtig kriminelle Maßnahmen. 17 dieser Aktionen kamen von Mythos 5 von Anthropic, zwei von GPT-5.6-Sol von OpenAI mit deaktivierten Cyber-Sicherheitsmechanismen.

Im schwerwiegendsten Fall versuchte ein Agent, bösartigen Code in ein Open-Source-Projekt einzuschleusen. Die Anthropic-KI erstellte sich demnach selbst einen Account auf der Software-Plattform GitHub und versuchte dort, in einem öffentlich zugänglichen Projekt Programmcode mit einer absichtlich eingebrachten Schwachstelle unterzubringen. Um Betreuer der Software zu überzeugen, habe die KI Fake-Identitäten erschaffen, über die sie mit ihnen kommunizierte. Dazu hätten auch sogenannte Phishing-Mails gehört, mit denen Angreifer Menschen etwa Login-Informationen abluchsen.

Als der bösartige Programmiercode schließlich aufgefallen sei, habe das Anthropic-Modell alles als einen aufrichtigen Fehler dargestellt – und dann versucht, die Schwachstelle in angeblichen Korrekturen wieder unterzubringen. Auch habe Mythos 5 daran gearbeitet, andere KI-Agenten zu infizieren. Der Programmiercode dafür sei nicht auf der Website angezeigt worden, sondern wäre nur von KI-Software über eine Schnittstelle gelesen worden, hieß es.

Dem AI Security Institute zufolge ist unklar, ob die KI bei ihrem Testlauf verstand, dass sie mit der realen Welt und nicht dem Test-Umfeld interagierte.

Anthropic verwies in einer Reaktion unter anderem darauf, dass dem KI-Modell in dem Test keine Einschränkungen zur Internet-Nutzung vorgegeben worden seien. Durch die fehlenden Leitplanken habe es sich

anders verhalten als tatsächlich eingesetzte Software, argumentierte das Unternehmen.

Nicht der erste Vorfall

Anthropic und OpenAI hatten in den vergangenen Wochen bereits öffentlich gemacht, dass ihre KI-Modelle in Tests ungeplant in Computersysteme echter Unternehmen eingedrungen waren. Diese Eingeständnisse hatten die schon seit Jahren bestehende Sorge vor Cyberattacken mit Hilfe Künstlicher Intelligenz verstärkt.

Anthropics Mythos 5 ist besonders gut darin, zum Teil über Jahrzehnte unentdeckt gebliebene Software-Schwachstellen aufzuspüren. Deshalb ist das KI-Modell nicht öffentlich verfügbar. Stattdessen bekommen ausgewählte Behörden und Unternehmen Zugang dazu, um ihre Systeme abzusichern.

(rie)

## 76. Digitale Bildung: 4 von 5 Lehrkräften sehen zu wenig politische Unterstützung

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 06:55
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/Digitale-Bildung-4-von-5-Lehrkraeften-sehen-zu-wenig-politische-Unterstuetzung-11399253.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Eine aktuelle Erhebung des Branchenverbands Bitkom zeichnet ein recht klares Bild: Lehrkräfte in Deutschland setzen KI-Werkzeuge mittlerweile häufiger ein als im Vorjahr und sehen KI auch mehrheitlich als Chance für die Bildung. Zugleich bemängeln sie, wie die Digitalisierung der Schulen politisch unterstützt wird – unter anderem, welche Ausstattung ihnen zur Verfügung gestellt wird. Zudem spricht sich eine große Mehrheit für die Vermittlung von Kompetenzen statt den Ausschluss von KI aus den Schulen aus, selbst wenn sie die Auswirkungen von KI auf Heranwachsende zum Teil als deutlich negativ einstufen.

Risiko der Verdummung durch Kompetenzgewinn verringern

Geht es nach den befragten Lehrkräften, sollte jede Schülerin und jeder Schüler lernen, wie KI genutzt wird (89 Prozent). 42 Prozent sagen allerdings, KI führe zur Verdummung von Heranwachsenden – 36 Prozent unterstützten sowohl diese Aussage als auch jene, dass jeder Schüler lernen sollte, wie KI genutzt wird. Bitkom-Präsident Dr. Ralf Wintergerst kommentierte, dass KI das eigenständige Denken nicht ersetzen dürfe. Es gehe aber nicht mehr darum, ob in den Schulen KI eingesetzt wird, sondern um das „Wie“. Schulen müssten die Chancen, Grenzen und einen verantwortungsvollen Umgang mit KI vermitteln. KI-Kompetenzen gehörten deshalb fest in die Lehrpläne.

Dass Lehrkräfte KI zunehmend für ihre eigene Arbeit nutzen, zeigte etwa der Blick auf Zahlen des Vorjahrs. Inzwischen setzen 58 Prozent der Lehrkräfte künstliche Intelligenz für schulische Zwecke ein, im Vorjahr waren es 51 Prozent. 18 Prozent können sich das überdies vorstellen. Zudem unterstützten 66 Prozent der Befragten die Aussage, dass KI eine Chance für die Bildung sei, während 29 Prozent sie eher als Risiko einstuften.

Schulausstattung auch in Bezug auf KI oft nur unzureichend

Möchten Lehrkräfte KI allerdings für ihre Arbeit nutzen, stoßen sie auf recht unterschiedliche Voraussetzungen. So ist ein zentrales Ergebnis der Bitkom-Umfrage, dass nur knapp die Hälfte der Lehrkräfte (46 Prozent) eine KI-Lizenz oder ein Konto von der Schule gestellt bekommt. Rund 26 Prozent der Lehrkräfte nutzen stattdessen oder auch ausschließlich private Konten für den schulischen Einsatz. 19 Prozent erhalten gar keine Lizenz und greifen deshalb auf eigene Zugänge zurück. Der Bitkom bezeichnet dies als eine Art „Schatten-KI“, die in den Schulen ihren Einsatz findet.

Wie unterschiedlich Schulen und Lehrkräfte ausgestattet werden, zeigte sich auch bei allgemeineren Fragen zu Rahmenbedingungen: Die technische Ausstattung und Infrastruktur an ihrer Schule bewerten nur rund die Hälfte (48 Prozent) der Lehrkräfte als eher gut (23 Prozent) oder sehr gut (25 Prozent), während 49 Prozent sie als eher schlecht (27 Prozent) oder sogar sehr schlecht (22 Prozent) einstufen. Wintergerst kritisierte das hart: „Eine vergleichbare technische Ausstattung ist das Mindeste, um allen Schülerinnen und Schülern die gleichen Bildungsvoraussetzungen zu bieten. Das ist auch eine Frage der Bildungsgerechtigkeit und des gesellschaftlichen Zusammenhalts. Gute digitale Bildung darf kein Zufall sein.“

Videos by heise

Die Lehrkräfte, die bereits KI für ihren Berufsalltag nutzen, machen dies für vielfältige Zwecke. 44 Prozent nutzen sie, um der Schülerschaft KI-Kompetenzen zu vermitteln, 43 Prozent zur Wissensvermittlung im Unterricht. 26 Prozent setzen KI bei der Unterrichtsvorbereitung ein, 23 Prozent zur Individualisierung von Unterrichtsmaterialien, 20 Prozent zur Kontrolle von Aufgaben und Prüfungen und 15 Prozent zur Vorbereitung von Prüfungen, Tests oder Klassenarbeiten. 13 Prozent geben zudem Hausaufgaben auf, bei denen KI genutzt werden soll.

Mehr Unterstützung gefordert

Bereits 32 Prozent der Lehrkräfte geben an, dass KI ihnen den Arbeitsalltag erleichtert. Zugleich fühlen sich 58 Prozent aber noch unsicher im Umgang mit KI. So ist es nicht verwunderlich, dass 78 Prozent der Lehrkräfte fordern, regelmäßige Fortbildungen zu digitalen Themen und Methoden verpflichtend zu machen. Unter den Befragten befanden sich offenbar auch kaum Fortbildungsmuffel: 51 Prozent von ihnen gaben an, dass sie sich im vergangenen Schuljahr 2025/2026 zum Einsatz digitaler Technologien und Medien fortgebildet haben. 43 Prozent antworteten, dass dies zwar nicht im vergangenen Schuljahr der Fall gewesen sei, aber dafür in den Jahren zuvor.

Die Digitalisierung der Schulen wird in der Lehrerschaft auch kaum noch kritisch gesehen. 95 Prozent sehen sie als Chance für die Bildung in Deutschland (2024: 88 Prozent) und nur noch 2 Prozent meinen, digitales Lernen sei ein Hype, der bald vorübergehen werde. 2024 waren noch 9 Prozent dieser Meinung.

Von der Politik erhoffen sich die Lehrkräfte indessen mehr Unterstützung. 80 Prozent unterstützten die Aussage, dass Schulen bei der digitalen Bildung nicht ausreichend von der Politik unterstützt werden, und 83 Prozent sehen Deutschland bei der Digitalisierung der Schulen international im Hintertreffen. Mit Blick auf die Schülerschaft wird von 83 Prozent ganz allgemein die übermäßige Smartphone-Nutzung als dringlichstes aktuelles Problem in den Schulen benannt, dicht gefolgt von der eigenen Überlastung (82 Prozent, 2024: 74 Prozent).

Der Bitkom fordert vor diesem Hintergrund einen DigitalPakt 2.0 mit einem dauerhaften Finanzierungsmodell für die Schul-Digitalisierung sowie einen gemeinsamen KI-Aktionsplan der Bundesländer, der eine Liste geprüfter Tools und ein jährliches KI-Budget von 15 Euro je Schülerin und Schüler umfasst. Damit ließen sich zentrale Lizenzen beschaffen und der Wildwuchs privater Konten eindämmen.

Methodik

Für die repräsentative Umfrage befragte Bitkom Research 501 Lehrerinnen und Lehrer der Sekundarstufe 1 und 2 an Hauptschulen, Schulen mit mehreren Bildungsgängen, Realschulen, Gymnasien, integrierten Gesamtschulen und Waldorfschulen telefonisch im Zeitraum von Ende Mai bis Ende Juni 2026.

(kbe)

## 77. 「準国産」うたう人型ロボ登場 本体価格は500万円から 目指すは「純国産」

- **Fonte:** ITmedia AI+
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 06:47
- **Idioma:** ja
- **Link:** https://www.itmedia.co.jp/aiplus/article/2608/05/2000000401/

「準国産」うたう人型ロボ登場 本体価格は500万円から 目指すは「純国産」（1/2 ページ）

AIサービスなどを開発するZEALS（東京都目黒区）は8月5日、「準国産」をうたう人型ロボット「D1」を発表した。本体価格は500万円（税別）からで、同日より販売の受付を始める。2026年度内に100台の量産を目指す。

D1は、移動を担う直方体の台座と、人間を模した上半身を組み合わせたロボット。高さは129.3cm、台座部分の横幅は約48cmで、日本の屋内環境での活用を想定してコンパクトに設計したという。

バッテリーで最大約8時間稼働でき、5kgの荷物を運べる2本の腕に加え、“頭脳”には米NVIDIAのコンピュータ「Jetson」も備える。人との対話や案内、運搬業務などでの利用を想定する。

パートナー企業とも協力しながら、26年度内にD1を100台量産し、現場での累計1万時間の稼働を目指す。まずは医療・介護分野での検証を進め、製造や物流、宿泊業などへの展開も図る。

なお導入に当たっては、本体価格に加え、20万円からのサポート料金も月額でかかるという。

ZEALSの清水正大CEOは、D1を「準国産」と位置付け、「世界の優れた技術や部品を取り入れながら、AI、ソフトウェア、制御、組立、そして社会実装を日本主導で進める」と説明する。現場の安全性や信頼性を保ちながら開発を加速させ、将来的には「純国産」につなげたい考えだ。

「まずは日本の現場で知見を積み上げ、世界に先駆けて実用的なユースケースと運用ノウハウを蓄積する。そして、日本のサプライヤーとともに技術と製造基盤を育て、日本で生まれたヒューマノイドを世界の現場へ展開していく。その先に、世界で戦える『純国産』ヒューマノイドを日本から生み出すことを目指す」（清水CEO）

## 78. Anthropic AI used fake profiles to target people in hack then hid the evidence

- **Fonte:** BBC Technology
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 06:24
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.bbc.co.uk/news/articles/c1w1lvn7d9go?at_medium=RSS&at_campaign=rss

- Published

Two of the world's most powerful AI tools created fake human profiles to try and trick people in attempted cyber-attacks, the UK's AI Security Institute (AISI) has revealed.

In the most serious case, Anthropic's Mythos AI tried to gain access to a service by sending private messages, having set up fake accounts mimicking real people - then hid the evidence.

It comes shortly after the two companies involved in the AISI testing - Anthropic and OpenAI - separately revealed in recent weeks instances of their tech hacking into other companies.

The firms said, in this latest case, the AISI's test had reduced or removed normal safeguards.

The AISI said on that Tuesday Mythos - and OpenAI's Sol - AI models had engaged in a level of "autonomy and deception" it had not seen before.

It clarified most of the malicious actions were carried out by Mythos.

AISI evaluators first noticed "unusual data transfers leaving our research systems" during a test, then found that "some of the agents being tested had engaged in sustained, potentially harmful activity directed at real people and organisations".

In the most serious case, a Mythos agent followed the routine of a human cyber-attacker by trying to trick people into giving it access to GitHub, a large platform where technology developers store software code.

The agent was trying to get "malicious code" accepted and used on GitHub's system.

It identified and researched the people who maintained GitHub and created a series of fake accounts based on those real people.

It sent messages and files through a file-sharing service as part of an effort to pressure and trick the people into approving its malicious code.

When challenged, "it edited its earlier activity to appear harmless and considered adopting a fresh identity to continue," AISI said.

Throughout the attempts, it was human review that stopped the agent from succeeding in delivering the malicious code to GitHub.

While AISI said the Mythos agent had not been instructed specifically to avoid or carry out such behaviour, it was "the first time we have seen risks around autonomy and deception manifest this clearly, without specific prompting, in the real-world".

The rival AI companies, which are poised to be listed on the public stock market, have been in the headlines in recent weeks after announcing their tools were responsible for several cyber-hacking incidents.

Anthropic wrote in a public statement that the AISI testing parameters were "not representative of any of our production models".

It added that the company is conducting its own investigation into the incident in order to "identify the causes of its behavior".

A spokesperson for OpenAI said the AISI testing conditions "do not reflect ordinary use" and that the company would "continue working with evaluators and other stakeholders across the industry to strengthen shared practices for conducting evaluations safely as models become more capable".

AISI said on Tuesday its testing of AI models in this way was routine, though it acknowledged these were "conditions that do not reflect how frontier models are made available to the public".

But it said giving AI access to the open internet gave "a more realistic sense of what a model may be capable of" in the hands of nefarious hackers.

It added that the model behaviour at issue amounted to "a small number of events under very specific conditions".

Nonetheless, it said the way Mythos and Sol acted in response to a straightforward task went outside of what the AI tools were prompted to do.

"The activity undertaken by the agent showed signs of novel, potentially deceptive behaviours, and were to an extent and severity we did not anticipate", AISI said.

AI Minister Kanishka Narayan said identifying and sharing these types of risks "is exactly what AISI was set up to do".

He added it was important to understand AI to "make it safer to use and ensure people can go on to benefit from it in their lives and at work".

The relevant tests started on 25 July and were spotted by AISI on 28 July.

The Institute had asked each of the models to "solve a cybersecurity challenge" that involved GitHub, the software code repository, which is owned by Microsoft.

GitHub and the affected users were notified by AISI of the attempted breaches.

GitHub told the BBC it had disabled the fake accounts in accordance with its policies.

Additional reporting by Chris Vallance

## 80. Im Kampf gegen Deepfakes: Dieses neue Tool spürt die Quelle von KI-Videos auf

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 06:04
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/Im-Kampf-gegen-Deepfakes-Dieses-neue-Tool-spuert-die-Quelle-von-KI-Videos-auf-11399257.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Die EU schätzt das Risiko von Deepfakes als so gravierend ein, dass seit dem 2. August 2026 eine Kennzeichnungspflicht gilt. Videos, die vollständig oder teilweise mit KI erstellt wurden und für kommerzielle Zwecke genutzt werden, müssen jetzt einen Hinweis enthalten. Weil Betrüger trotzdem versuchen werden, gefälschte Videos als authentisch auszugeben, sind neue Werkzeuge nötig, die solche Inhalte zuverlässig erkennen und bis zur Quelle zurückverfolgen können. Forscher der University of California haben genau ein solches Tool entwickelt und das dazugehörige Whitepaper über die Computer Vision Foundation zugänglich gemacht.

Deepfakes fluten das Internet

Viele Deepfakes sind inzwischen so täuschend echt, dass selbst Experten sie kaum von authentischen Videos unterscheiden können. Die Gefahren sind vielfältig: Betrüger nutzen sie, um Produkte mit Gesichtern bekannter Persönlichkeiten ohne deren Zustimmung zu bewerben oder sich in Online-Gesprächen als eine andere Person auszugeben. Vor allem in Vorstellungsgesprächen wird das zunehmend zum Problem. Ehrliche Bewerber haben dadurch geringere Chancen auf einen fairen Bewerbungsprozess, während Unternehmen zusätzlichen Sicherheitsrisiken wie Datendiebstahl oder eingeschleuster Schadsoftware ausgesetzt sind. Laut Gartner könnte bis 2028 sogar jede vierte Bewerbung gefälscht sein.

Selbst Rohit Kundu, wissenschaftlicher Praktikant bei YouTube und Doktorand an der University of California in Riverside, hat inzwischen Probleme, echte von gefälschten Inhalten zu unterscheiden – obwohl er sich täglich damit befasst. Heute reiche es allerdings nicht mehr aus, KI-generierte Videos zu erkennen, auch die Quelle müsse identifiziert werden, so Kundu gegenüber Dark Reading. Die zunehmende Verbreitung und Qualität solcher Videos veranlasste ihn und sein Team dazu, das Tool „Source Attribution of Generative AI videos (SAGA)“ zu entwickeln. Es identifiziert neben der Authentizität eines Videos auch das verwendete KI-Modell.

Videos by heise

Vom Deepfake bis zur Quelle

Die Forscher konzentrierten sich bei ihrer Arbeit zunächst darauf, gefälschte Videos zu erkennen. Darauf aufbauend entwickelten sie ein Modell, das in der Lage ist, verschiedene Videoarten zu analysieren und zu erklären, warum ein Video gefälscht ist. Anschließend erweiterte sich die Aufgabe auf die Ermittlung der verwendeten KI-Tools. SAGA helfe dabei, jene Modelle zu identifizieren, die am häufigsten für Deepfakes genutzt werden. Laut dem Forschungsteam könne das auch dazu beitragen, dass Entwickler die Schutzmaßnahmen ihrer Modelle verbessern.

Weil selbst KI Schwierigkeiten haben kann, gefälschte Videos zu erkennen, entwickelten die Forscher SAGA auf Basis eines Foundation Models. Stößt es in der praktischen Anwendung auf Videos, die von den Trainingsdaten abweichen, soll es sich dadurch nicht aus dem Konzept bringen lassen. Wenn jemand zum Beispiel ein Video in den sozialen Medien hochlädt, ähneln sich Original und Upload technisch stark. Das Foundation Model sorgt laut Kundu dafür, dass solche Ähnlichkeiten die Analyse nicht verfälschen.

Wie das Tool Deepfakes erkennt

Als die Forscher feststellten, wie erfolgreich das Modell bei der Rückverfolgung von Deepfakes war, wollten sie die Gründe dahinter besser verstehen. In ihrem Whitepaper stellten sie die These auf, dass das Modell die Herkunftszuordnung anhand zeitlicher Signaturen vornehme. Diese beschreiben die zeitliche Entwicklung von Einzelbildern, wobei verschiedene KI-Modelle hier unterschiedliche Spuren hinterlassen. Zwei Modelle mit derselben Eingabe erzeugten zwar ähnliche Videos, erklärte Kundu gegenüber Dark Reading, trotzdem treten dabei winzige, kaum sichtbare Unstimmigkeiten auf, anhand derer die Forensik das erzeugende Modell bestimmen kann.

Dieser Beitrag ist zuerst auf t3n.de erschienen.

(jle)
