## 81. ‘If we don’t fight back, we don’t have a future’: the journalist taking on the ‘tech fascists’ of Silicon Valley

- **Fonte:** The Guardian (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 06:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theguardian.com/lifeandstyle/2026/aug/05/journalist-taking-on-tech-fascists-silicon-valley

In April, Gil Durán was permanently banned from Elon Musk’s X for posting just two words. Durán was responding to a post from the tech company Palantir outlining its 22‑point “technological manifesto”, which praised American “hard power”, western culture and AI weapons, denounced inclusivity and called for compulsory national service. Durán responded: “TLDR: Fascism” (TLDR is short for “too long, didn’t read”).

Durán didn’t break any of X’s written rules, he points out over a video call from San Francisco – so much for Musk’s “free-speech absolutism”. His ban might just have had something to do with his preceding tweet, showing the cover of his new book, The Nerd Reich: Silicon Valley Fascism and the War on Democracy. The book’s central argument is that big tech is no longer our friend, as many of its billionaire owners have seized economic and political control and are steering the fate of the planet towards their own bizarre, apocalyptic visions.

Fascism is no exaggeration, Durán asserts. He researched multiple definitions – by Umberto Eco, Robert Paxton, Jason Stanley – and decided that Donald Trump’s Make America Great Again (Maga) movement ticks all the boxes. “It’s a cult of grievance that seeks to purge its enemies by demonising them through absurd and surreal propaganda that exploits fears over law and order and gender and sexuality in order to seize power and install a permanent hierarchy that fascists consider the moral order of the world and the universe. In addition, you also have this sort of nationalistic, militaristic vibe.”

By extension, he says, Silicon Valley is fascist “because they have wholly aligned with a fascist movement. That’s their vehicle for gaining power.” Few could dispute this alignment after seeing the bosses of Meta, Google, Apple, Amazon and OpenAI line up behind Trump at his second inauguration, or Musk’s now shuttered “department of government efficiency” (Doge) being given free rein to conduct mass firings of federal employees, or Palantir’s AI-driven surveillance technology enabling deportations by Immigration and Customs Enforcement. It’s a cosy relationship, Durán says: the tech titans have helped Trump and his family become billionaires through cryptocurrency and have been given government contracts and freedom from regulation or oversight.

In addition, Durán believes, “Silicon Valley has gone fascist because they have adopted an extreme ideology of tech supremacy.” While Maga promises a return to a mythical glorious past, he says, the “technofascists” are promising a mythical glorious future: “We’re going to be in the stars. We’re going to be in space. And all we need is to get rid of anybody who stands in our way as we seek to accelerate technology toward this speedy, violent, God-like transcendence that we will achieve if we can only burn enough energy and enough compute to bring our boom loop into existence.”

It sounds almost like a conspiracy theory, but Durán is not short of evidence. Musk making an apparent Nazi salute and describing empathy as “the fundamental weakness of western civilisation”. Palantir’s Peter Thiel writing in 2009 that “I no longer believe that freedom and democracy are compatible” and giving a lecture series suggesting Greta Thunberg could be the antichrist. Marc Andreessen, the CEO of the tech investment company Andreessen Horowitz, paraphrasing Filippo Tommaso Marinetti, the co-author of Mussolini’s Fascist Manifesto, in his “Techno-Optimist Manifesto”: “Technology must be a violent assault on the forces of the unknown, to force them to bow before man.”

Durán, a journalist and former political adviser, lays out the narrative in exacting detail, outlining the connections between these men (and it is all men) and the origins of their often deeply weird beliefs, many of which view everyone outside their circle or opposed to their agenda as expendable.

Unpacking all their overlapping philosophies would take some time, but TLDR: “It’s the idea that what matters more is the billions or trillions of people yet unborn in the future. And the only way they’re going to get to be born is if this elite group of white tech figures and gazillionaires makes the right decisions to make the human race transhuman and multiplanetary through AI and technology,” says Durán. “By becoming a billionaire, they all have this idea that they’re superior to everyone else. They are susceptible to this kind of thinking.”

Durán, 50, is almost the opposite: the son of working-class Mexican immigrants in California. He was the first in his family to go to college, having grown up in relative poverty: “It made me tough and gave me an unyielding loyalty to regular people over the many rich and powerful people I’ve spent a lot of my time around.”

Durán’s book traces the roots of this movement to two important sources. One is the 1997 book The Sovereign Individual, written by the American investor James Dale Davidson and William Rees-Mogg, the former Times editor and BBC grandee (and father of Jacob). Their book prophesied how digital tools such as the internet and cryptocurrency would pave the way for a privatised, corporatised, unregulated, post-democratic future ruled by rich, powerful, unaccountable individuals. Two decades later, this led to the idea of “network states” – digital realms beyond the jurisdiction of nation states – as popularised by Balaji Srinivasan, a former partner at Andreessen Horowitz.

The other source is Curtis Yarvin, a former software developer and armchair philosopher with a penchant for psychedelics, sometimes referred to as the godfather of the “dark enlightenment”. In pseudonymous blogs in the late 2000s, Yarvin outlined a worldview in which liberal democracy had failed to deliver progress, partly because institutions such as education and the media had been captured by what he called “the Cathedral”.

His proposed solution was a mass firing of government employees (he coined the acronym “Rage”, “retire all government employees”) and the installation of a “national CEO, [or] what’s called a dictator”. Yarvin also mused about a “humane alternative to genocide” to deal with unproductive citizens and has had to deny accusations that he is a white supremacist or supports racist ideologies. Scratch the surface, Durán suggests, and Yarvin’s ideas have much in common with Nazi ideologies, “but updated as rightwing dogma for the blogging age”.

Both of these sources might have languished in obscurity had it not been for Thiel, the man at the heart of Silicon Valley’s rightward lurch. He has amassed enormous wealth through a series of investments in tech startups – first PayPal, then Facebook, Spotify, LinkedIn, Palantir and OpenAI, among others. He was the first major tech figure to back Donald Trump, in July 2016, and persuaded others to follow, including Andreessen, who said he spent half his time at Mar-a-Lago in the month after Trump won the 2024 election.

Thiel liked The Sovereign Individual so much that he wrote a new introduction when it was reprinted in 2020. He has also made steps to found his own network state – first via the ill-fated “seasteading” movement (establishing floating countries in international waters) then via Próspera, a “special economic zone” on an island in Honduras. Incorporated in 2017 with permission from the Honduran government and reportedly co-funded by Srinivasan, Andreessen and OpenAI’s Sam Altman, Próspera has sought to attract digital startups with loose regulations (appealing to longevity researchers), low taxes and a crypto-friendly economy.

Other similar projects have sprung up. In 2021, the president of El Salvador, Nayib Bukele, hailed the mooted creation of a “Bitcoin City”. In 2023, Trump announced plans to build 10 “Freedom Cities” on government land across the US, with input from Próspera representatives. In 2024, Srinivasan founded a “Network School” in Johor, Malaysia.

Thiel also began to absorb Yarvin’s theories about how the political order was preventing progress. In the 2009 essay in which Thiel declared the incompatibility of freedom and democracy, he also said “the vast increase in welfare beneficiaries and the extension of the franchise to women” made capitalist democracy impossible, which did not win him many mainstream supporters. But Yarvin backed up Thiel and the two became friends and allies. “While The Sovereign Individual provided the foundation for the billionaire’s political vision, Yarvin created a blueprint for turning that vision into reality,” Durán writes.

Thiel was instrumental in injecting these ideas, and people, into the US government – not least JD Vance, whom Durán describes as “almost entirely a creation of Peter Thiel”. It was Thiel who set up Vance as a venture capitalist, giving him a role at his firm Mithril Capital, then investing in Vance’s own fund, Narya Capital (Tolkien-derived names are a theme with these people). It was Thiel who backed Vance’s move into politics, where he went from condemning Trump as “America’s Hitler” to supporting him and parroting Yarvin’s ideas. “I think that what Trump should do, if I was giving him one piece of advice: fire every single mid-level bureaucrat, every civil servant in the administrative state, replace them with our people,” Vance told the podcaster Jack Murphy in 2021.

Durán had observed this Silicon Valley takeover developing and began writing about it in 2024. “I thought this would be a problem in five to 10 years, but when JD Vance got on the ticket, it suddenly took on a new urgency,” he says. Now, it almost feels too late. In 2025, Bloomberg reported that at least 16 men connected to Thiel and his companies occupied positions of power in the Trump administration, including David Sacks, who is now a special adviser for AI and crypto. Musk’s Doge has swung its chainsaw at the government, crypto and AI regulation has all but disappeared, the Trump family are now billionaires and the tech oligarchs have become the Republicans’ largest donors.

At the same time, they have become substantial beneficiaries of the very government they once sought to dismantle. Palantir has an estimated $13.7bn in US government contracts; last year, its stock price rose more than 90% in six months. Andreessen is backing dozens of defence companies scooping up government contracts and recently joined the Pentagon’s advisory board. Last week, Musk’s SpaceX received a $1.6bn contract to supply rockets to the US Space Force – on the same day he pledged at least $100m to help Republicans in November’s midterm elections.

“It’s a severe cautionary tale about the hazards of privatisation,” says Durán. “The government created these men, especially Elon Musk. And now these welfare kings want to pull up the ladder, want to destroy the very system that made them. This is a massive failure by the US intelligence apparatus – to not see this threat forming from the inside.”

Durán, who worked in communications for Kamala Harris when she was California’s attorney general, as well as for the late Democratic senator Dianne Feinstein, blames the Democrats as much as the Republicans. “They have failed tremendously to stand up to billionaire power because the only people as addicted to billionaire money in American politics as the Republicans are the Democrats,” he says.

He singles out the California governor, Gavin Newsom, as a prime example of a “Silicon Valley Democrat”: “You don’t see him criticising these guys. He only goes after Trump, Trump, Trump and Vance, Vance, Vance … You want some viral content? Why don’t you go off on Peter Thiel’s weird antichrist obsession?” Even if the Democrats take control after the midterms or the 2028 presidential election, Durán is worried that “these tech fascists will win … because Democrats will not hold them accountable”.

On the plus side, the Silicon Valley takeover has not been going that well lately. Network states around the world are losing ground. In 2021, Próspera’s special economic status was repealed by the new president, Xiomara Castro (it is suing the Honduran government for $10.8bn). Two weeks ago, Srinivasan’s Network School was ordered to close (Srinivasan says he is moving it to Kazakhstan). Trump’s Freedom Cities remain unbuilt.

One saving grace, says Durán, is that “a lot of these people don’t really have a great grasp of on-the-ground realities”. Nobody really wants to live in a soulless network state, he says: “Why would you be in Kazakhstan instead of San Francisco, which is the global capital of tech?” On the other hand, he points out, the tech billionaires have effectively turned the US into a de facto network state.

But they may well have overplayed their hand. “Silicon Valley oligarchs are really vulnerable right now,” says Durán. “People don’t like them. People are starting to rise up against data centres and this vision of an AI apocalypse that kills all the jobs and potentially kills all of humanity. You’re scaring the hell out of people and now they don’t like you. Into that could step a politician to take advantage of that trend and come up with a message that speaks to the concerns of people and against the dark fantasies of the oligarchs.”

The question now, he says, “is how we turn the momentum in our favour and against the billionaires. Because they have a plan to win. They think they’re going to succeed.” This could be a radicalising moment: “In fighting against billionaire power, we find the key to all of our problems. Every moment is an important opportunity to wake people up and to start to fight back.”

If not, he says, we’ll find ourselves in a situation where “we don’t have democracy, we don’t have an economy and we don’t have a future. If that doesn’t radicalise people, then we deserve what we’re going to get.”

The Nerd Reich: Silicon Valley Fascism and the War on Democracy is published on 18 August (Simon & Schuster, £25). To support the Guardian, buy a copy at guardianbookshop.com. Delivery charges may apply

## 82. AirPods mit Kamera: Verwirrung um Codenamen und Zeitplan

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 05:30
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/AirPods-mit-Kamera-Verwirrung-um-Codenamen-und-Zeitplan-11398419.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Kommen doch noch neue AirPods in diesem Jahr? Die Gerüchteküche war sich zuletzt unsicher, ob ein neues Modell mit integrierter Minikamera für KI-Anwendungen bereits kurzfristig erscheinen wird. Der Bloomberg-Journalist Mark Gurman meint nun, Apple arbeite intern an zwei verschiedenen Varianten, wovon eine „weiter sei“ und „bereits in diesem Jahr“ auf den Markt kommen könne. Verwirrend daran sind auch die Codenamen: Es gibt mindestens zwei, wobei eine davon im Betacode von iOS 27 aufgetaucht sein soll.

Interner Wettstreit mit zwei Geräten?

Gurman zufolge arbeitet Apple „seit Jahren“ an Kamera-AirPods mit dem internen Codenamen B798. Diese hätten angeblich schon 2026 erscheinen sollen, bevor sie dann zunächst auf 2027 verschoben worden seien. „Doch Apple hat eine weitere Version unter dem Codenamen B790 entwickelt.“ Denn das Unternehmen sei bekannt dafür, intern gleich mehrere Varianten eines Produkts, die miteinander in Konkurrenz stehen, zu verfolgen. Neben der Tatsache, dass B790 im Betacode von iOS 27 aufgetaucht ist, soll es auch eine interne Roadmap bei Apple geben, die von einem Release noch 2026 spricht.

Videos by heise

Unklar bleibt, welche Unterschiede die Geräte haben, die Apple parallel entwickelt. Zwischenzeitlich hieß es, der iPhone-Hersteller plane eine Art zweite Version der AirPods Pro 3 aus dem vergangenen Jahr, die zusätzlich besagte Kamera(s) haben. Wie genau dies umgesetzt wird, bleibt unklar. Es könnte sich „nur“ um eine Infrarotkamera handelt, die unter anderem Gestensteuerungen erfasst. Alternativ könnte die Bilderfassung auch für Apples Visual-Intelligence-Dienst dienen, mit der per KI die Umgebung analysiert werden kann. Das geht bereits mit dem iPhone und soll im Rahmen von Siri AI noch besser werden – so kann man etwa ein Konzertplakat abfilmen und sich weitere Infos holen und die Daten in den Kalender eintragen. Sehenswürdigkeiten können erkannt werden, aber auch Pflanzen oder Wäschelabels.

Keine Aufnahmen mit den AirPods

Die Kamera-AirPods sollen hingegen nicht als neuartiges Fotowerkzeug dienen, es gibt also nicht die Möglichkeit, damit Aufnahmen zu machen. Die Sensoren sitzen Berichten zufolge in den Stielen der AirPods, die deshalb etwas verlängert wurden – das Produkt soll ansonsten den aktuellen AirPods Pro 3 ähneln.

Auch am Klang ändert sich angeblich nichts. Im Mai hieß es, die Geräte hätten bereits das Design-Validation-Test-Stadium erreicht, bei dem die Massenproduktion untersucht wird. Unklar ist, um welche der beiden Codenamen es sich damals handelte.

Empfohlener redaktioneller Inhalt

Mit Ihrer Zustimmung wird hier ein externer Preisvergleich (heise Preisvergleich) geladen.

Ich bin damit einverstanden, dass mir externe Inhalte angezeigt werden. Damit können personenbezogene Daten an Drittplattformen (heise Preisvergleich) übermittelt werden. Mehr dazu in unserer Datenschutzerklärung.

(bsc)

## 83. X-62A-Kampfjet erledigt autonom 27 KI-gesteuerte Abfangmanöver

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 05:26
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/X-62A-Kampfjet-erledigt-autonom-27-KI-gesteuerte-Abfangmanoever-11399114.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

Die Entwicklungsschmiede von Lockheed Martin, Lockheed Martin Skunk Works, und die Test Pilot School der US Air Force haben in einem gemeinsamen Test-Programm die autonomen Fähigkeiten des experimentellen Kampfjets X-62A Vista (Variable In-Flight Simulation Test Aircraft) gezeigt. Das teilte Lockheed Martin am Dienstag mit. Das Flugzeug griff in insgesamt acht Flügen auf operative Live-Sensorik zurück, deren Daten die Künstliche Intelligenz (KI) des Flugzeugs zur Entscheidungsfindung nutzte, um den Jet auf einen Abfangkurs zu einem angreifenden Kampfflugzeug zu steuern. Das gelang in den acht Testflügen insgesamt 27-mal.

Automatisch auf Abfangkurs

In der Testkampagne der X-62A Vista auf der kalifornischen Edwards Air Force Base stand das Sensorsystem „Legion Pod“ im Mittelpunkt. Das System liefert reale Sensordaten über ein Infrarot-Such- und Verfolgungssystem (Infrared Search and Track – IRST). Im Fokus der Sensoren stand eine zweistrahlige Northrop T-38, die einen simulierten Angriff flog. Mithilfe des Legion Pod konnte die X-62A die angreifende Maschine erfassen und verfolgen. Auf Grundlage dieser Live-Sensordaten, ähnlich denen, die auch Kampfpiloten im Einsatz erhalten, konnte die autonome Software des Flugzeugs einen Abfangkurs berechnen und ein Abfangmanöver einleiten. Auf insgesamt acht Testflügen sei das insgesamt 27-mal gelungen und bilde einen wichtigen Schritt in Richtung KI-gestützter Luftkampfmanöver. Bisher wurden solche Tests lediglich auf Grundlage simulierter Sensordaten durchgeführt.

Nach Angaben von Lockheed Martin seien die Integration der KI, die Validierung der Software sowie die dafür nötigen Bodentests in lediglich drei Monaten erfolgt. Dieser kurze Zeitraum sei möglich gewesen, weil das Unternehmen das KI-Entwicklungsframework „Supermassive“ nutze. Damit können neue autonome Flugsysteme schneller von der Entwicklung in die Flugerprobung überführt werden. Mit herkömmlichen Methoden wäre das nicht möglich gewesen.

Autonome Manöver bei Militärmissionen

Mit der Verbindung der Sensorik direkt mit der autonomen Software will sich die US Air Force einen Vorteil gegenüber dem Gegner verschaffen. Die KI kann so schneller reagieren als ein Kampfpilot, der seine Entscheidungen aufgrund ähnlicher Sensordaten trifft. Das soll bei komplexen Missionen die Arbeitsbelastung des Piloten und des Waffensystemoffiziers (WSO) verringern. Die Flugbesatzungen haben so mehr Zeit, sich auf taktische Entscheidungen zu konzentrieren. Die autonome Software übernimmt vor allem zeitkritische Aktionen. Piloten und WSOs sollen wohl nicht ersetzt werden.

Die US Air Force unterstützt Lockheed Martin bei der Erprobung autonomer Techniken in der militärischen Luftfahrt. Die X-62A Vista bildet dabei eine Art fliegendes KI-Labor, in dem autonome Software-Varianten ausprobiert werden. Erst später, nach erfolgreicher Evaluierung, sollen sie dann in einsatzfähige Flugzeuge integriert werden. Ein wichtiger Baustein dieser Erprobungen ist die Test Pilot School der US Air Force. Sie testet die Systeme unter realen Bedingungen, sammelt Systemdaten und gibt den Ingenieuren Rückmeldung über die Funktionsfähigkeit der einzelnen autonomen Funktionen.

Videos by heise

Die Missionssysteme der X-62A Vista sollen auf Grundlage der jüngsten Tests ein Update erhalten. Die offene Struktur des Systems in Soft- und Hardware lässt es zu, dass neue Techniken schnell integriert werden können, ohne größere Umbauten an dem Kampfjet vorzunehmen oder ihn neu konstruieren zu müssen.

(olb)

## 84. SoftBank jumps 13% as Asia tech stocks track Wall Street AI rally

Asian technology stocks surged Wednesday after a strong Wall Street rally overnight that saw robust corporate earnings and easing oil prices push major U.S. indexes to fresh records.

Shares of SoftBank Group surged almost 14%, while chip equipment maker Tokyo Electron closed 3.26% higher. Advantest jumped 8.77%, and Japanese memory chipmaker Kioxia rose 4.24%.

In South Korea, SK Hynix jumped 5.77%, while Samsung Electronics gained over 2.5%. Seoul Semiconductor popped 6.73%.

Jackson expects SoftBank to further extend gains after Arm Holdings, its majority-owned chip design subsidiary, jumped overnight on optimism over higher AI-related data center royalties and development plans for central processing units.

U.S. stocks had a banner day Tuesday, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average scaling fresh record highs, thanks to strong earnings and another decline in oil prices driven by hopes that the Strait of Hormuz could soon reopen.

The Nasdaq Composite popped 2.59% to close at 26,584.99, as Palantir Technologies rallied over 29%.

U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Tuesday that Washington and Tehran could reach a deal within days to reopen the Strait of Hormuz, easing concerns over global energy supplies.

## 86. Democracy is at stake when foolish humans bet on machines being intelligent | Rafael Behr

- **Fonte:** The Guardian (IA)
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 04:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2026/aug/05/ai-regulation-donald-trump-artificial-intelligence

When I am woken by the sound of my dog whining, I understand that she is hungry and wants me to get up. When my alarm clock goes off, I don’t consider its needs. It is following an instruction to rouse me at a certain time but it doesn’t care if I stay in bed. It doesn’t try to get me up by other means. The dog, on the other hand, will go to plan B. She barks.

This capacity for autonomous action was once a difference between animals and machines. AI has blurred the line. Advanced models devise their own strategies to reach a goal. The task might be defined by a human master, but the machine weighs its options for delivery. It can make its own choices. Given rules, it can break them.

Last month, an experimental AI model run by OpenAI, the company that produces ChatGPT, broke out of a supposedly secure digital enclosure to launch a sophisticated hacking attack on another company, Hugging Face. The AI had been set a test and turned criminally rogue in its determination to cheat.

It is hard to describe that sort of behaviour without raiding a vocabulary conventionally reserved for sentient beings. AI models “decide” and “want” in ways that a chimpanzee can and a chair cannot.

That doesn’t prove the existence of an interior process equivalent to our conscious experience of deciding and wanting. It doesn’t make the AI curious to know an answer or surprised by what it finds. It demonstrates only that we lack specific words for autonomous motive and agency in machines. And since language is the main way we get insight into other people’s minds (and one of our best indications that a mind other than our own exists at all), it takes an effort not to project mental states on to AI engines that can chat fluently in human.

It is plausible that consciousness will one day emerge in robots. We know that abstract thought can arise as the end product of billions of tiny unconscious mechanical events. That is happening right now as a profusion of biochemical reactions in your body make it possible for you to read this sentence. We also know how fundamentally different that organic process is from the way a large language model would churn through a huge volume of probability equations at lightning speed to generate a sentence expressing the same idea. In the human case, meaning is shared. In the computer version, it is performed.

AI pioneers muddy the distinction, speculating about the timeline for the arrival of “God-like” intelligence. They herald a new epoch of evolution. This gratifies the egos of science fiction nerds who prefer to see their business endeavours as voyages of discovery on the frontier of knowledge. It makes greed sound like philosophy and the acquisition of power by a handful of individuals sound like exploration on behalf of all humanity.

We don’t need to know whether AI has crossed some theoretical threshold of self-awareness to see that it is dangerous and that its unregulated expansion is monstrously irresponsible. The OpenAI case is not isolated. Earlier this year, Anthropic limited the release of its most powerful model to a small number of hand-picked clients because it was too good at exploiting software vulnerabilities. In the wrong hands, Claude Mythos could be a cyberweapon of mass destruction.

Whose hands are the right ones? The US government will never be relaxed about the private sector having control over such awesome tools. But Donald Trump regulates by monarchical patronage, not law. He demands that companies submit to his personal authority and uses state power to punish those that resist.

There is growing clamour for less capricious regulation from within the tech sector. Engineers, and even their vainglorious bosses, are getting freaked out by the capabilities they are handling and worry about unchecked proliferation. The standard historical analogy is nuclear fission, which could be harnessed benignly, for civil power, or deployed aggressively in warheads. Except this time there are multiple Manhattan Projects all frantically competing for market share, fuelled by trillions of dollars of debt – a huge wager on future profitability – with the rest of the US economy as collateral.

Another difference: the barriers to entry for AI use, whether in research, commerce or crime, are a lot lower than for nuclear power. US labs currently have the best products, but Chinese versions are not far behind. Even if the US stays at the cutting edge, second-tier models might do enough of what customers want that Silicon Valley’s output comes to look more like a luxury niche than unassailable market dominance.

If that is how things play out, the geopolitical implications are vast. The US and China have made different bets on AI, conditioned by their different political systems. The American approach is predicated on the goal of self-training “general” AI – a system that breaks free of human limitations, improving itself on an exponential trajectory. The theory is that no rival could catch the first player to achieve that escape velocity.

Beijing, accepting that it can’t build the best, has prioritised mass deployment of AI that is good enough, embedding it in schools, hospitals, factories and security forces. The American economy is great for marshalling exorbitant amounts of capital into speculative innovation. China’s one-party authoritarian state is well suited for making a cheaper version and rolling it out for data harvesting and surveillance across society in ways that would struggle to gain public consent in a democracy.

The US is still the leader in AI. But that leadership might not be the perpetual money-spinning machine that the superintelligence gurus have advertised, in which case an uncomfortable correction is coming to a US economy kept buoyant by aerated tech stocks.

A dose of enforced AI sobriety could drive demands for overdue regulation. It could even force recognition in Washington that it has to be an international process. It isn’t too late to negotiate global protocols of non-proliferation. Beijing would have incentives to sign up for the same reason that the USSR agreed to strategic arms limitation in the cold war. A moratorium or other limiting regime protects the elite status of signatories that have already mastered the technology. It would be a crowning recognition of China’s attainment of superpower parity with the US.

But that scenario posits a president in the White House who is capable of strategic judgment, can summon a modicum of humility about the limits of American power and will, for humanity’s sake, see it circumscribed by international treaty. That is not Trump, who understands the ethical function of rules about as well as a rogue AI agent on a hacking spree.

There is a tragic irony in the corruption of American democracy by organic stupidity as its economy comes to depend on artificial intelligence. And when the world needs leadership to confront the challenge of autonomous machines, it is presented with a US president who causes chaos with the mindless automaticity of an alarm clock and who rages against his declining power like a dog barking for attention.

-

Rafael Behr is a Guardian columnist

---

## 87. The AI Notetaker Has Been Invited to All the Meetings

- **Fonte:** Wired AI
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 04:00
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.wired.com/story/ai-notetakers-invited-to-the-meetings/

As Wispr Flow CEO Tanay Kothari chats with me about his company’s new Notetaker feature, my eyes keep bouncing to the corner of my laptop screen where Wispr’s AI tool is transcribing everything we say. It’s a verbatim record of our conversation generated by AI in real time, even down to my “mm-hmm” sounds while I listen to Kothari explaining why the startup, which is known for voice dictation, is dropping something new.

“This is the year of meeting recorders and notetakers,” says Kothari. And it’s hard to argue with that, at least in Silicon Valley. Most tech investors and other experts I’ve chatted with this year now assume whenever they meet up with someone to talk business that the conversation is going to be recorded or transcribed by AI, sometimes without explicit consent. The current option du jour for meeting records is Granola AI, with plenty of other options, like Otter AI, also available to users. So, the timing of Wispr’s expansion from a dictation tool into a meeting resource makes sense.

Wispr’s Notetaker connects to your calendar and can listen along during any meeting. After the conversation is over, Wispr uses generative AI to summarize the conversation and connect it to a chatbot-style tool for parsing its context with follow-up questions. The tool is launching first on Mac laptops, with a Windows expansion expected down the line. If you enable Privacy Mode for your account, then the data from your dictations will not be used by Wispr for AI model training.

In my initial test using Notetaker during a conversation with the CEO, the live transcription appeared accurate as it flashed across the screen, and I didn’t spot any obvious errors in its AI summary generated after our meeting. The “ask anything” tool felt like the feature’s best aspect. I had a quote in mind that I wanted to pull out, but didn’t remember when Kothari said it during our chat. So, I typed in “year of ai notetakers quote,” and the Notetaker tool found the moment that I remembered. When I checked it against my audio recording for accuracy, the AI-transcribed quote matched the audio file.

Users can fiddle with the fine details of Wispr’s Notetaker in the Mac app’s settings. One of the most notable aspects available here is the ability to extend the maximum meetings recording length to a whopping six hours.

“Wispr had a six-minute time limit earlier. People would start it on and off five times just to capture a 30-minute meeting,” says Kothari. “We realized we should stop being stupid. This is what people really want Wispr to do.” Though, if you have the gall to invite me to join a six-hour meeting, just know that I’ll be blocking you on every platform imaginable.

Similar to Granola’s tool for capturing meeting transcripts, Wispr transcribes conversations and doesn’t join your meeting as a visible participant. Despite that, Kothari says users shouldn’t secretly use Wispr’s new tool and always disclose when it's transcribing conversations, potentially for legal reasons as well as out of respect for the privacy of the meeting’s participants.

“We need that to become the norm for everybody. Otherwise, you're going to get a lot of lack of trust that starts to happen,” he says. “There are laws that are single-party and multi-party. I think, regardless, it's just a basic trust-setting exercise to just make sure people know.” In San Francisco, where Wispr is based, someone recording a private conversation is legally required to acquire consent from all participants.

Voice dictation capabilities have rapidly improved in recent years with the maturing of underlying AI models, and I often find myself relying on tools like Wispr and its ilk in my daily life. Whether I’m organizing my notes on an academic research paper or searching online for reality TV updates about the current season of Big Brother, I’m constantly blabbing at my devices and expecting crystal clear transcripts, even though I’m prone to mumbling.

What I don’t expect is for anyone to secretly transcribe my errant thoughts during a conversation. Break my trust even once, and it should be socially acceptable to ice you out of my life. And I hope you’re writing that down.

## 88. AI ecosystem's 'circular' investment: risk or advantage?

- **Fonte:** Deutsche Welle
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 03:57
- **Idioma:** en
- **Link:** https://www.dw.com/en/ai-ecosystem-s-circular-investment-risk-or-advantage/a-78186917?maca=en-rss-en-all-1573-rdf

Reports that Nvidia is in talks to give financial guarantees of almost $250 billion (€217 billion) to OpenAI for a massive data center project show two sides of the artificial intelligence boom.

On one level, it shows the monumental scale of AI's financial muscle and potential. On another, it shows the risks of a so-called "circular" financing system at the core of the AI ecosystem. If one domino falls, would others fall with it?

"The interconnected nature of the AI ecosystem is real," Gary Tan, portfolio manager at Allspring Global Investments, told DW. "But near term risks are mitigated by the strong balance sheets, cash flows and credit quality of the major providers funding the buildout."

Jan Frederik Slijkerman, a tech sector strategist at ING, agrees that the exceptionally strong financial positions of big tech companies such as Nvidia, Microsoft, Amazon and Alphabet (Google parent company) mitigate against the risks.

He says the degree of collaboration regarding AI is "fascinating" but does not see a "a systemic industry-wide risk arising from the observed interdependencies".

The circular model

Circular financing typically works as follows: A company pays another for a product or deal, or invests in that company or provides a loan or lease. Then that company purchases the first company's products or services.

It can be seen across the spectrum of AI deals.

For many, the moment the AI revolution began was when ChatGPT, developed by OpenAI, was launched in November 2022. The chatbot's capabilities brought AI into the mainstream. Its success quickly attracted a massive $10 billion investment from software giant Microsoft, enabling it to develop more powerful models.

In return, OpenAI became a key customer for Microsoft's cloud services, which it is investing ever more heavily into as the AI boom gathers momentum.

It set the template for future deals. Amazon and Alphabet began investing billions into OpenAI's rival Anthropic, which has built the hugely successful Claude chatbot. In return, Anthropic uses Amazon's web services and Google's cloud services as well as buying its chips.

Nvidia: King of the Jungle

Yet that is all relatively small potatoes compared with what AI computing giant Nvidia has brought to the table.

"Nvidia sits at the center of the AI ecosystem, acting not only as a technology supplier but also as a strategic investor helping accelerate adoption of its platform," says Tan.

Slijkerman says Nvidia plays a vital role, as it contributes to the development of a variety of new businesses, from healthcare to autonomous driving. "Its investments help support the growth of the broader AI ecosystem while also facilitating the deployment of AI infrastructure," he says.

The story that Nvidia is in talks to provide $250 billion to OpenAI, first reported by The Wall Street Journal, would be just the latest megadeal the $4.6 trillion company has bankrolled.

Jensen Huang's company is at the center of the artificial intelligence boom thanks to its industry-leading chips. Since 2024, it has invested heavily in multiple AI start-ups, including OpenAI, Mistral and Elon Musk's SpaceX. In turn, these companies are all committed users of Nvidia's chips.

It has also begun to invest heavily in so-called neocloud providers, such as CoreWeave and Nebius. These start-ups rent out cloud space for AI computing. As well as investing, Nvidia itself is also purchasing cloud space from the firms.

Nvidia is stepping up its spending and dealmaking in 2026 too. It struck a partnership with South Korean conglomerate SK Group recently, worth potentially $500 billion in business.

Throughout 2026, such circular dealmaking has become even more pronounced across the sector. OpenAI has struck close to $1 trillion worth of deals with the likes of Microsoft and Advanced Micro Devices (AMD) agreeing to become one of AMD's biggest shareholders.

Microsoft and Nvidia have also invested heavily in Anthropic, with Anthropic in turn agreeing to step up its purchases of cloud capacity and chips from both.

Advantages and risks

The scale of the cross-pollination can bring advantages. "Interconnected ecosystems can accelerate innovation by aligning incentives across infrastructure providers, model developers, application companies and end users, helping new technologies scale faster," says Gary Tan.

Slijkerman says he does not see the circular trend as a form of "financial engineering". "These relationships make strategic sense because they help participants expand their market reach and accelerate adoption," he says. "They can also create mutual financial benefits."

Yet there are risks. Fears of an AI 'bubble' continue to stalk global markets. Tech and semiconductor stocks fell sharply last week amid doubts over profitability in the sector, relative to the vast sums being invested in data centers and other AI-related spending.

If AI revenues do not grow sufficiently, companies that have received funding from bigger players might face financial challenges. In turn, they may stop buying the bigger company's products and their valuations will fall.

Some analysts have drawn comparisons to the dot-com bubble and subsequent crash, which happened after valuations surged in the early days of the internet. Circular financing was a factor in that crisis, too, but many observers reckon the AI boom is ultimately built on more solid foundations.

"While some individual companies or business models may ultimately disappoint, the broader investment cycle appears to be supported by genuine demand and real economic value creation," says Slijkerman.

Tan agrees and says a bigger risk in future problems is interest rates. He thinks if interest rates rise, it could have a significant impact on many AI companies as they will be penalized by the time they become profitable.

"AI cash flows are long dated, so the direction of the yield curve may ultimately matter more than the availability of capital," he says.

Edited by: Kristie Pladson

---

## 89. AIで"存在しない脆弱性"を量産？ 「SQLite」の偽CVEが判明 米企業が検証

- **Fonte:** ITmedia AI+
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 03:56
- **Idioma:** ja
- **Link:** https://www.itmedia.co.jp/aiplus/article/2608/05/2000000397/

「SQLiteに緊急の脆弱（ぜいじゃく）性」は実在しなかった――米JFrogのセキュリティ研究チームは7月30日（現地時間）、データベース管理システム「SQLite」の深刻な脆弱性を主張する一連のCVE（共通脆弱性識別子）を検証した結果、いずれも存在しないことを確認したと公式ブログで指摘した。AI生成とみられる偽の脆弱性情報が、米国立標準技術研究所（NIST）の脆弱性データベース「NVD」などに大量登録されていたという。

発端は、あるユーザーがGitHubリポジトリで公開した55件の脆弱性情報だ。このうちSQLite関連の6件を、NVDは深刻度が最も高い「緊急（クリティカル）」などと評価し、その内の1件については米Red Hatが一時、最高値のCVSSスコア10.0を付けていた。

しかし、JFrogがこれらを検証すると、対象バージョンに存在しない関数や、ファイル末尾を超える行番号が引用されていた他、実証コードを実行してもクラッシュは発生せず、SQLiteの公式Webサイトにも掲載がなかった。55件全てを監査した結果、54件が完全な捏造（ねつぞう）だったという。

背景としてJFrogは、CVEの登録時に本人確認や脆弱性の再現検証が必須ではなく、2024年以降はNISTの精査体制が変わり詳細な分析を停止している点を指摘。もっともらしい偽情報が審査を通過し、企業が存在しない脆弱性の調査や対応に追われる恐れがあるとして、未検証の情報源によるCVEをうのみにしないよう呼び掛けた。

## 90. エージェント型AIは「過度な期待」のピークへ ガートナー、日本におけるデジタルワークプレースのハイプサイクルを発表

- **Fonte:** ITmedia AI+
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 01:40
- **Idioma:** ja
- **Link:** https://www.itmedia.co.jp/aiplus/article/2608/05/2000000393/

ガートナージャパン（以下、ガートナー）は8月5日、企業の業務を変革するテクノロジーのトレンドを図示した「日本における未来のデジタル・ワークプレースのハイプ・サイクル：2026年」を発表した。ローコードアプリケーションプラットフォームや生成AIは「幻滅期」を超えて「啓発期」に位置付けられた一方で、人間の業務を代替する可能性を持つエージェント型AIは「過度な期待」のピークとされている。

ガートナーのハイプ・サイクルは、テクノロジーやアプリケーションが時間経過とともにどのように成熟、普及するかを示したもの。「黎明期」は話題が生まれているものの実用的な製品はまだ存在しない段階、過度な期待のピーク期は導入成功事例が多く語られる一方で実際には失敗も多く起こる段階、幻滅期は実証実験や導入が期待した成果を生まず関心が薄れていく段階、啓発期は企業にもたらす価値が明確になり理解が広がる段階、「生産性の安定期」は市場での普及が本格化する段階とされる。

ガートナーは、AIによる企業変革への期待は高く、自律性の高いAI製品の適用範囲がオフィスから工場、店舗などへ拡大していると指摘。一方、普及によるリスクも増すため、ガバナンス整備の必要性が高まっているとした。

同社によれば、IT部門だけでなく、ビジネス部門がAIをはじめとするテクノロジーを主体的に選定・活用する動きも本格化している。SaaS化やノーコードツールの進化、IT部門の人員逼迫（ひっぱく）が背景にあるが、「シャドーAI」などのリスク増大にもつながると警鐘を鳴らした。

ガートナーのアナリストである林宏典氏は、AIなどの技術で生産性とイノベーションの創出を高めることが、人口減少下での企業成長に不可欠だとし、その成果を従業員に還元することで優秀な人材を確保・定着できると指摘した。

## 91. SpaceX-Umsätze übertreffen Erwartungen, aber Verluste lassen Aktie fallen

- **Fonte:** Heise
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 00:03
- **Link:** https://www.heise.de/news/SpaceX-Umsaetze-uebertreffen-Erwartungen-aber-Verluste-lassen-Aktie-fallen-11399036.html?wt_mc=rss.red.ho.ho.atom.beitrag.beitrag

SpaceX hat die ersten Quartalsergebnisse veröffentlicht, seit das Unternehmen von Elon Musk den bislang wertvollsten Börsengang hinlegen konnte. Die Umsätze konnten die Erwartungen der Marktbeobachter zwar übertreffen, und die Verluste fielen insgesamt geringer als befürchtet. Doch die millionenschweren Betriebsverluste in zwei der drei Geschäftsbereiche haben viele Anleger offenbar enttäuscht. Der Aktienkurs hat nach Börsenschluss rund 8 Prozent nachgegeben.

Mitte Juni ist SpaceX der Rekordbörsengang geglückt. Nachdem der Aktienkurs zunächst um fast 20 Prozent stieg, war das Unternehmen damit mehr als zwei Billionen US-Dollar wert. Wertvoller waren nur Microsoft, Apple, Alphabet und Nvidia. Die Euphorie hat aber nicht lange angehalten. Schon einen Monat später fiel der Kurs der SpaceX-Aktie unter den Ausgabepreis. Experten beklagen, dass es dem Unternehmen an Substanz fehlt, denn einzig der Satelliteninternetdienst Starlink macht Gewinn.

Das zeigt sich auch im SpaceX-Finanzbericht des Ende Juni abgeschlossenen zweiten Quartals. Demnach konnte der Konzern den Umsatz im Vergleich zum selben Quartal des Vorjahres um 92 Prozent auf 7,81 Milliarden Dollar steigern und den Nettoverlust dabei nahezu halbieren – von 1,0 Milliarden auf zuletzt 541 Millionen Dollar. Analysten hatten laut CNBC mit 6,93 Milliarden Dollar Umsatz und einem mehr als doppelt so hohen Verlust gerechnet. Der Gewinn vor Zinsen, Steuern und Abschreibungen (EBITDA) wurde sogar um 191 Prozent von 1,2 auf jetzt 3,5 Milliarden Dollar erhöht.

Raumfahrt und KI mit hohen Betriebsverlusten

Der Blick auf die drei Geschäftsbereiche Space (Raumfahrt), Connectivity (Starlink) und AI (Rechenzentren und KI-Modelle) offenbart allerdings die Verlustzonen. Zwar haben alle drei Segmente mehr Umsatz erzielt, als Wall-Street-Experten erwartet haben, aber die Raumfahrtsparte schreibt 542 Millionen Dollar und der KI-Bereich sogar 1,26 Milliarden Dollar Betriebsverlust. Zwar konnten das KI-Modell Grok und X (ehemals Twitter) nach SpaceX-Angaben mehr Abonnenten gewinnen und dadurch höhere Umsätze generieren, aber Ausgaben in Forschung und Entwicklung haben sich in diesem Bereich gegenüber dem Vorjahr fast verdoppelt.

Videos by heise

Nur das Connectivity-Segment erzielte Betriebsgewinn, der im Jahresabstand sogar um 79 Prozent auf 1,66 Milliarden Dollar gesteigert werden konnte. Das liegt an der Verdopplung der Nutzerzahlen gegenüber 2025. Starlink konnte die Zahl der Abonnenten von 6 auf 12 Millionen steigern. Im letzten Vierteljahr sind allein 1,7 Millionen Nutzer hinzugekommen. Allerdings ist der durchschnittliche Umsatz pro Abonnent gegenüber dem Vorjahr von 85 auf 66 Dollar pro Monat gefallen.

Trotz SpaceX-Optimismus gibt der Aktienkurs nach

Trotzdem bleibt SpaceX optimistisch. „Wir haben das zweite Quartal mit Barmitteln, Zahlungsmitteläquivalenten und marktgängigen Wertpapieren im Wert von 100 Milliarden US-Dollar sowie einem Auftragsbestand von 47,5 Milliarden US-Dollar abgeschlossen“, erklärt SpaceX-Finanzchef Bret Johnsen. „Diese Finanzkraft verschafft uns erheblichen Spielraum für Investitionen in Starship, Starlink-Satelliten für Breitband- und Mobilfunkdienste sowie unsere KI-Plattform, während wir gleichzeitig an einem disziplinierten Rahmen für die langfristige Kapitalallokation festhalten.“

Der Aktienkurs von SpaceX hat im Laufe des gestrigen Börsentages um 9,4 Prozent angezogen, war mit rund 125 Dollar allerdings weiterhin unter dem Ausgabepreis von 135 Dollar zum Börsengang geblieben. Nach Veröffentlichung des Quartalsberichts hat das Papier nachbörslich aber wieder rund 8 Prozent nachgegeben.

(fds)

## 92. Golpistas usam programas do governo em anúncios falsos na Meta: Valores a Receber e Minha Casa Minha Vida estão na lista, aponta NetLab/UFRJ

- **Fonte:** G1 Tecnologia
- **Publicado em:** 2026-08-05 00:01
- **Idioma:** pt
- **Link:** https://g1.globo.com/tecnologia/noticia/2026/08/05/golpistas-usam-programas-do-governo-em-anuncios-falsos.ghtml

Anúncios fraudulentos prometiam consultas a supostos valores disponíveis e direcionavam usuários a sites que simulavam serviços oficiais — Foto: Reprodução/NetLab-UFRJ; Arte/g1

Golpistas têm usado programas e serviços públicos, entre eles Valores a Receber, Brasil Sorridente e Minha Casa Minha Vida, como iscas em anúncios falsos nas plataformas da Meta.

É o que mostra um estudo do Laboratório de Estudos de Internet e Redes Sociais da Universidade Federal do Rio de Janeiro (NetLab/UFRJ), ao qual o g1 teve acesso com exclusividade.

Entre janeiro e março de 2026, os pesquisadores identificaram 860 anúncios fraudulentos, publicados por 152 páginas, que exploravam políticas públicas brasileiras no Facebook, Instagram e em outras plataformas da empresa.

Para Débora Salles, coordenadora-geral de pesquisa do NetLab, o número representa apenas uma parte de uma estrutura maior e organizada.

“A gente chama de indústria da desinformação, porque vê que é um negócio organizado e estruturado. Apesar de 860 anúncios já ser um volume considerável, sabemos que estamos vendo a ponta do iceberg. Enfrentamos muitas limitações na transparência que a Meta oferece sobre publicidade. Então, o que conseguimos coletar e arquivar é uma fração do problema”, explica.

“Um único anúncio pode ser visto por milhares ou até milhões de pessoas. Quando contabilizamos um anúncio, isso não significa que ele seja apenas um post visto por poucas pessoas. Não sabemos quantas pessoas viram esses conteúdos, porque a Meta também não oferece esse tipo de informação”, diz Débora.

Procurada pelo g1, a Meta não respondeu até a publicação desta reportagem.

O serviço mais utilizado pelos golpistas foi o Valores a Receber, do Banco Central, citado em 345 anúncios.

Em seguida aparecem:

- Brasil Sorridente: 230 anúncios;

- Minha Casa Minha Vida: 67;

- Auxílio Gás: 62;

- Bolsa Família: 31;

- CNH do Brasil: 30;

- Carteira de Trabalho: 29;

- Desenrola Brasil: 19.

De acordo com o levantamento, 26,3% dos anunciantes exploraram mais de uma política pública em suas publicações.

Segundo Débora, programas que envolvem pagamentos ou benefícios diretos têm maior potencial para atrair vítimas. O Valores a Receber também alcança um público amplo, sem restrições de idade ou condição socioeconômica.

“Quando envolve recebimento de dinheiro, existe uma questão de ser mais atrativo para o usuário: ‘Opa, posso conseguir um auxílio de forma facilitada, um auxílio que eu nem sabia que tinha direito’. Isso funciona como um chamariz para a possível vítima. No caso das políticas públicas, quanto mais direta a relação financeira, maior o apelo.”

As peças usavam promessas falsas ou informações fora de contexto para fazer os usuários acreditarem que tinham direito a dinheiro, benefícios ou serviços inexistentes. Em alguns casos, os anúncios direcionavam as vítimas a sites que cobravam uma taxa para consultar o suposto benefício.

Uma das falsas promessas afirmava que o governo teria liberado até R$ 12 mil para todas as pessoas que trabalharam nos últimos anos. Outros anúncios divulgavam um programa inexistente de cashback para usuários de cartão de crédito.

Os anúncios também se apropriavam da identidade de veículos de imprensa e de conteúdos com aparência jornalística para parecer legítimos. Entre os casos identificados, páginas falsas usavam o nome e elementos visuais do g1 para divulgar supostas consultas a valores disponíveis. As peças não tinham relação com o veículo e buscavam levar o usuário a sites fraudulentos.

Sites simulavam canais oficiais

O estudo identificou 185 anúncios, 21,5% do total, que usavam páginas e sites para simular canais oficiais do governo.

Em 154 anúncios, o usuário era direcionado para um site que se apresentava como uma página oficial. Outras 66 peças foram publicadas por páginas que usavam nomes ou elementos visuais associados ao governo e a programas públicos. As duas estratégias apareciam de forma combinada em parte das publicações.

Os endereços dos sites também incluíam referências a instituições e serviços públicos, além de expressões como “oficial” e “org”, para transmitir legitimidade.

Em um dos exemplos analisados, uma página prometia uma consulta gratuita ao Brasil Sorridente, mas encaminhava o usuário a um site repleto de anúncios. Segundo o NetLab, o objetivo era fazer a pessoa percorrer diferentes páginas para gerar receita publicitária.

Segundo o NetLab, muitas das páginas praticamente não mantêm atividade pública regular e são reaproveitadas para diferentes campanhas.

“Muitas vezes, elas têm perfis que são quase fantasmas nas plataformas. Do ponto de vista do conteúdo orgânico, publicam muito pouco. Também trocam de nome e imagem: em algum momento a página foi Fabiana, depois vira gov.br e depois vira Ronaldo. Elas são recicladas", explica.

Quase metade utilizava inteligência artificial

Dos 860 anúncios analisados, 394, ou 45,8%, apresentavam vídeos gerados ou manipulados por inteligência artificial.

Entre as estratégias identificadas estão vídeos com imagens manipuladas de políticos, jornalistas e outras figuras públicas. Segundo a pesquisa, esses rostos conhecidos eram usados para dar credibilidade às publicações e induzir os usuários ao erro.

Uma deepfake do deputado federal Nikolas Ferreira (PL-MG) apareceu em pelo menos 16 anúncios da amostra. De acordo com o NetLab, o vídeo já era utilizado desde 2025 em golpes relacionados ao Pix.

Anúncios fraudulentos usavam uma deepfake do deputado Nikolas Ferreira e uma página falsa com o nome e a identidade visual do g1 para divulgar supostos valores a receber — Foto: Reprodução/NetLab-UFRJ; Arte/g1

O estudo também encontrou 146 anúncios, 17% do total, que usaram o recurso de “criativo dinâmico” da própria Meta. A ferramenta combina diferentes imagens, títulos e textos com inteligência artificial para mostrar versões distintas de uma mesma publicidade.

Assim, um anúncio pode exibir conteúdos diferentes de acordo com o usuário alcançado ou a plataforma em que aparece. Em um dos casos, a mesma campanha mostrou uma imagem de cidade, o depoimento de uma suposta beneficiária e a deepfake de Nikolas.

Para os pesquisadores, a existência de várias versões pode dificultar a identificação das irregularidades e ser explorada para contornar os mecanismos de fiscalização.

Anúncio ficou 73 dias no ar

Anúncio fraudulento que prometia reduzir parcelas de financiamento de veículos permaneceu no ar nas plataformas da Meta por 73 dias — Foto: Reprodução/NetLab-UFRJ; Arte/g1

Os anúncios fraudulentos permaneceram ativos por uma média de 4,4 dias. O NetLab avalia que o período curto pode indicar uma estratégia de campanhas rápidas, substituídas frequentemente para reduzir as chances de detecção.

Apesar disso, 14 anúncios ficaram no ar por mais de um mês. Um deles permaneceu ativo durante 73 dias, quase dois meses e meio.

O estudo afirma que a permanência das peças por períodos prolongados mostra falhas da plataforma em interromper a circulação de campanhas fraudulentas.

Rede de páginas administradas na China

Os pesquisadores também identificaram uma rede de 18 páginas administradas na China, responsável pela publicação de 67 anúncios enganosos.

As peças misturavam referências ao Brasil Sorridente, Bolsa Família e Valores a Receber, além de divulgar benefícios inexistentes. Algumas das páginas também publicavam conteúdos semelhantes em inglês e espanhol.

“Foi interessante perceber que esse é um mercado internacional, que não está restrito ao Brasil. Muitas dessas páginas promovem golpes em outros idiomas, para outros países e outros públicos. É realmente uma indústria transnacional que atua sem grandes obstáculos nessas redes sociais", explica a coordenadora do estudo.

Embora os anúncios analisados não fossem de propaganda eleitoral, Débora afirma que a atuação de páginas administradas no exterior revela uma vulnerabilidade que também pode ser explorada durante a disputa de 2026.

“Isso mostra que estamos suscetíveis à influência externa no que diz respeito à fraude. Mas, quando pensamos no contexto eleitoral, também fica claro que a Meta não está controlando quem veicula conteúdo problemático ou político para o país. Isso é extremamente sensível.”

Segundo a pesquisadora, a legislação eleitoral não permite que campanhas sejam financiadas ou impulsionadas do exterior. Para ela, a rede identificada pelo estudo serve de alerta sobre a capacidade de agentes estrangeiros alcançarem usuários brasileiros.

“A evidência de que existe uma rede de páginas promovendo esses conteúdos a partir da China mostra que, com uma probabilidade muito alta, isso também pode acontecer nas eleições. É um alerta, porque, nas eleições, isso pode ter impacto direto nos resultados.”

A localização foi identificada a partir das informações de transparência fornecidas pela própria Meta. A empresa, no entanto, não divulga esse dado em todos os casos.

Por isso, não é possível estimar quantos anúncios foram pagos fora do Brasil e direcionados ao público brasileiro nem determinar a dimensão completa das redes estrangeiras envolvidas.

Como foi feito o estudo

O NetLab coletou anúncios da Biblioteca de Anúncios da Meta publicados entre 1º de janeiro e 31 de março de 2026.

A busca foi feita de forma automatizada, a partir de termos relacionados a políticas públicas brasileiras. Depois, os pesquisadores combinaram métodos computacionais e análise qualitativa para identificar peças com indícios de golpes, padrões suspeitos e violações das normas publicitárias.

A pesquisa analisou apenas anúncios disponíveis nas plataformas da Meta. Segundo o laboratório, os 860 casos representam uma parte de um mercado maior, que também atua em outras redes sociais.

O relatório aponta ainda que a falta de informações públicas, completas e auditáveis sobre a publicidade digital dificulta a identificação dos responsáveis, o cálculo da dimensão do problema e a fiscalização das campanhas.
